The clause has **two limbs** and both are taught in full: (i) lessons from the lives and teachings of great leaders, reformers and administrators; (ii) the role of family, society and educational institutions in inculcating values. ### CURRENT-AFFAIRS ANCHOR

📰 The DoPT page for the National Programme for Civil Services Capacity Building (NPCSCB, "Mission Karmayogi") states that the programme builds **functional and behavioural competencies**, targets the citizen–government interface, and shifts the service from a rules-based to a roles-based logic through the **FRAC** approach (Framework of Roles, Activities and Competencies) and the **iGOT-Karmayogi** platform. Cabinet approval was on **2 September 2020**; the **Capacity Building Commission** was established on **1 April 2021**. These are dated figures for that release, not an undated programme total.

Mission Karmayogi is the only current Union instrument that treats **value and behaviour formation in a serving adult** as a designed institutional process rather than as something assumed to have already happened in childhood. It is therefore the live test of the Second ARC's own warning (paragraph 2.7.2) that such attitudes "are not skills which could be easily imbibed after joining the civil services."

**Limit of the anchor:** ⚠️ Users, courses and completions measure **reach**, not internalisation. Do not assert a general APAR or promotion linkage without a service-specific DoPT order naming the scope.

### SESSION 1 — What a "Human Value" Actually Is, and Why the Syllabus Puts It First

**Session 1 of 22 · Stage: Foundation · Syllabus limb: the concept "human values" that both limbs presuppose**

#### VISUAL FIRST

```text
              THE VALUE LADDER — FROM FEELING TO ENFORCEABLE STANDARD
              ========================================================

  LEVEL 5   LAW / RULE / CODE      "you must"        external, enforceable,
            (CCS Conduct Rules)                       sanction attached
                 ^
                 | codified into
  LEVEL 4   NORM                   "we do"           social expectation,
            (propriety, custom)                       stigma attached
                 ^
                 | shared and enforced by a group
  LEVEL 3   VALUE                  "this is worth"   ENDURING, TRANS-SITUATIONAL
            (honesty, compassion)                     -- THE SYLLABUS UNIT
                 ^
                 | stabilised by repetition + example
  LEVEL 2   ATTITUDE               "I favour X"      OBJECT-SPECIFIC, changeable
            (pro/anti a scheme)                       -> owned by Topic 03
                 ^
                 | organised from
  LEVEL 1   BELIEF + FEELING       "I think / feel"  momentary, unstable

  READ UP  = how a private feeling becomes an enforceable public standard.
  READ DOWN= why a printed rule fails when the value under it was never formed.

  THE EXAM TEST: an answer that treats levels 2, 3 and 5 as the same word
  cannot explain why a served officer obeys the rule and still fails the value.
```

*What the visual shows:* a value sits **above** attitude and **below** codified law. That single placement decides most of this topic. It explains why the syllabus asks about *inculcation* (you cannot legislate a level-3 item into existence) and why it asks about *exemplars* (levels 3 and 4 move by example, not by notification).

#### Core teaching

⚠️ **Working definition.** A **value** is an enduring belief about what is desirable or worthwhile that guides choice **across** situations. Three features do the analytical work: it is *enduring* (unlike a mood), it is *trans-situational* (unlike an attitude, which attaches to one object), and it is *evaluative* (it ranks options rather than merely describing them).

⚠️ **The distinction the examiner actually tests.** A value is trans-situational; an **attitude** is object-specific. A trainee can hold a warm attitude towards a cleanliness campaign and still falsify an attendance register, because the attitude was attached to one object and honesty as a *value* was never formed. Topic 03 owns attitude in full; this topic owns the level above it.

⚠️ **Adjacent terms, kept separate.** A **norm** is a shared social expectation enforced by approval and stigma; a **virtue** is a settled excellence of character that reliably produces valued action; an **interest** is what benefits me; an **aptitude** is a capacity to perform (Topic 04). Values can conflict with interests, and the whole of GS-IV Section B exists because they do.

✅ **Why the ARC treats values as the *content* of law rather than its opposite.** The Second ARC states at paragraph 2.2.1 that "Ethics provides the basis for the creation of such laws and rules. It is the moral ideas of people that give rise to and shapes the character of laws and rules. Our legal system emanates from a shared vision of what is good and just." Values are therefore upstream of legislation, not a soft alternative to it.

✅ **Why values cannot be legislated into existence.** The same report, at paragraph 2.2.4, concedes that "A system of laws and rules, however elaborate, cannot provide for all situations", and that "the higher the echelon in public service, the greater is the ambit of discretion." Discretion is precisely the gap that only an internalised value can fill.

✅ **The report's own bluntest formulation.** The Preface states: "Clearly, the absence of rules is not the problem. One cannot mandate honesty. The rule of law can only defeat the perverse mind. However, it cannot defeat the perversity of the heart."

⚠️ **The syllabus logic, stated once.** If values sit above attitude and below law, then the two printed limbs of this clause are not two topics but one argument: values must be **formed** (family, society, education — because law cannot form them) and they must be **modelled** (leaders, reformers, administrators — because a standard that nobody has been seen to keep is not believed).

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE

> A value is not a warm feeling about a good thing; it is an enduring, trans-situational standard that decides conduct exactly where the rulebook runs out — which is why the Second ARC concedes that "one cannot mandate honesty" while insisting that law nevertheless "emanates from a shared vision of what is good and just".

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

`enduring, trans-situational standard` · `value vs object-specific attitude` · `norm, virtue, interest, aptitude` · `ARC 2.2.1 — moral ideas shape the character of laws` · `ARC 2.2.4 — rules cannot provide for all situations` · `higher echelon, greater ambit of discretion` · `one cannot mandate honesty` · `formation limb and exemplar limb are one argument`

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH: claim -> named evidence -> analysis -> qualification

**Claim:** Human values matter to administration precisely because they operate where rules cannot reach.
**Named evidence:** The Second ARC's Fourth Report, *Ethics in Governance* (January 2007), states at paragraph 2.2.4 that "a system of laws and rules, however elaborate, cannot provide for all situations", and that "the higher the echelon in public service, the greater is the ambit of discretion"; its Preface adds that "one cannot mandate honesty."
**Analysis:** Discretion is the structural residue that codification leaves behind, and it grows with seniority; what fills that residue is not a further rule but an internalised standard of what is worth doing, which is why the syllabus asks how such standards are *inculcated* rather than how they are *notified*.
**Qualification:** This does not license the opposite error of treating values as a substitute for rules — the same report insists that ethical conduct requires "sanctions against their violations" and "competent disciplinary bodies", so the correct formulation is values *in* institutions, not values *instead of* institutions.

#### ADMINISTRATIVE APPLICATION IN INDIA

⚠️ A Sub-Divisional Magistrate deciding a discretionary land-conversion application faces no rule that names this applicant. The Conduct Rules tell her what she may not accept; they cannot tell her whether to prioritise revenue, an ecologically fragile catchment or a landless claimant's livelihood. The ranking she applies is her value hierarchy operating in the discretionary residue that ARC 2.2.4 describes. The auditable proxy is not her sincerity but her **recorded reasons**.

#### UPSC TRAP

❌ Defining a value as "a good moral quality such as honesty". → That is an example, not a definition, and it collapses value into virtue. The examiner is testing whether you can say *enduring* and *trans-situational*, because only those two words explain why a person with an excellent attitude to one scheme can still be dishonest in another file.

#### RECAP

Value = enduring, trans-situational evaluative standard. It sits above attitude (object-specific) and below codified rule (enforceable). ARC 2.2.1 makes values upstream of law; ARC 2.2.4 and the Preface make them indispensable downstream of law, in the discretionary residue. Both syllabus limbs follow from that placement.

### SESSION 2 — Origin and Timeline: How India Built an Apparatus for Inculcating Values

**Session 2 of 22 · Stage: Foundation · Purpose: date every institutional claim and prevent anachronism**

#### VISUAL FIRST

```text
   CONCEPTUAL ORIGIN (undatable in the modern sense — state it, do not date it)
   ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   * "Ethics" <- Greek ethikos = "arising from habit"   (ARC 1.2, verbatim)
   * Exemplar-based moral teaching is older than any Indian statute: the ARC's
     own Preface reaches for Confucius, Plato and Solzhenitsyn to make the point.
   * MINIMUM DATING RULE: never say a classical teacher "recommended" a modern
     institution. Kautilya diagnosed an incentive problem; he did not design a CVC.

   INSTITUTIONAL TIMELINE (each item dated from a source read for this review)
   ==========================================================================
   1869-1920 | Conduct prohibitions issued piecemeal under the Fundamental Rules
             | and Civil Service Regulations: habitual lending/indiscriminate
             | borrowing (1869), gifts (1876), buying-selling property (1881),
             | commercial investments (1885), promoting companies (1885),
             | post-retirement commercial employment (1920)      [ARC 2.7.3]
       |
       v
   1930s     | "Do's and don'ts" compendium collectively called Conduct Rules
   1947      | Prevention of Corruption Act — a new set of offences created
   1955      | The compendium converted into distinct rules
       |
       v
   1964      | SANTHANAM COMMITTEE (Committee on Prevention of Corruption) --
             | asks for "ways and means ... to ensure that idealism and patriotism
             | have the proper place in the ambition of our youth"   [ARC 2.7.1]
   1964      | CCS (Conduct) Rules, 1964 — the Santhanam enlargement  [ARC 2.7.4]
       |
       v
   1976      | 42nd Amendment inserts Part IVA, Article 51A: TEN Fundamental
             | Duties, on the Swaran Singh Committee's recommendation
       |
       v
   2002      | 86th Amendment: Article 21A (elementary education as a
             | fundamental right) AND the ELEVENTH duty, Article 51A(k) --
             | the parent's duty to provide education to a child aged 6-14
       |
       v
   2006      | 1 September: National Colloquium, "Ethics and Governance --
             | Moving from Rhetoric to Results"; ARC Chairman M. Veerappa Moily's
             | speech quotes five Arthasastra propositions  [Annexure-I(2)]
   2006      | Draft Public Service Bill lists generic civil-service "values"
       |
       v
   2007      | JANUARY: 2nd ARC, 4th Report, *Ethics in Governance* --
             | Box 2.2, Box 2.7, Box 2.8; paras 2.7.1-2.7.6 on inculcation
       |
       v
   2020      | 2 September: Union Cabinet approves Mission Karmayogi (NPCSCB)
   2021      | 1 April: Capacity Building Commission established
   2026      | 6 February: PIB reports 1.49 cr users, 4,342 courses,
             | 7.26 cr completions, 23 languages  (DATED FIGURES, NOT A TOTAL)
```

*What the visual shows:* India did not move from "values" to "institutions"; it moved from **prohibition** (1869–1964) to **constitutional duty** (1976, 2002) to **designed adult behaviour formation** (2020–). Each shift is a different theory of how a value gets into a public servant.

#### Core teaching

✅ **The conceptual origin, stated rather than dated.** ARC 1.2 records that "ethics" is "from the original Greek term *ethikos*, meaning 'arising from habit'." A habit is built by repetition inside a community — which is exactly why the syllabus names family, society and educational institutions as the sites of inculcation.

✅ **1964, Santhanam.** The ARC quotes the Committee on Prevention of Corruption at paragraph 2.7.1: *"For a country like India, development of her material resources and raising the standards of life of all classes are, indeed imperative. At the same time, the deterioration in the standards of public life has to be arrested. Ways and means have to be found to ensure that idealism and patriotism have the proper place in the ambition of our youth. The lack of moral earnestness, which has been a conspicuous feature of recent years, is perhaps the greatest single factor which hampers the growth of strong traditions of integrity and efficiency."* This is the earliest post-independence official statement that value formation in **the young** is an administrative-reform problem, not only a family one.

✅ **1976 and 2002, the constitutional turn.** The 42nd Amendment inserted Part IVA and Article 51A, specifying **ten** Fundamental Duties on the recommendation of the Sardar Swaran Singh Committee; the **86th Amendment (2002)** added the **eleventh**, Article 51A(k). The Constitution thus contains an explicit, textual answer to the syllabus's second limb — and it names the family (51A(k) addresses the parent or guardian) and the ideals of the freedom struggle (51A(b)).

✅ **The status caution that must accompany it.** The constitutional source is explicit that the Fundamental Duties are **non-justiciable**: "The Constitution does not provide for their direct enforcement by the courts. Moreover, there is not legal sanction against their violation. However, the Parliament is free to enforce them by suitable legislation." An answer that treats Article 51A as an enforceable obligation is factually wrong.

✅ **Why the duties were framed as value education rather than as commands.** Indira Gandhi's quoted justification, recorded in the same source, is that "the moral value of fundamental duties would be not to smoother rights but to establish a democratic balance by making the people conscious of their duties equally as they are conscious of their rights." Note the printed form `smoother` in the source; it is preserved rather than silently corrected.

⚠️ **What the timeline proves for an answer.** The Indian state has repeatedly discovered that prohibition does not produce probity. Each new instrument — conduct rules, constitutional duties, capacity-building programmes — is an attempt to reach *earlier* in the causal chain. That trajectory is the single most useful analytical frame available for any "role of institutions in inculcating values" question.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE

> India's answer to value formation has migrated steadily upstream — from prohibiting specific acts (gifts in 1876, property dealings in 1881), to enlarging conduct rules after Santhanam in 1964, to writing eleven non-justiciable duties into Article 51A, to designing adult behavioural competencies under Mission Karmayogi from 2020 — because each stage discovered that the previous one arrived too late.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

`ethikos = arising from habit` · `Santhanam Committee, 1964 — idealism and patriotism in the ambition of our youth` · `CCS (Conduct) Rules, 1964` · `42nd Amendment, 1976 — Part IVA, Article 51A, ten duties` · `Swaran Singh Committee` · `86th Amendment, 2002 — Article 21A and the eleventh duty 51A(k)` · `non-justiciable, no legal sanction` · `National Colloquium, 1 September 2006` · `Ethics in Governance, January 2007` · `Mission Karmayogi, 2 September 2020` · `Capacity Building Commission, 1 April 2021`

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH: claim -> named evidence -> analysis -> qualification

**Claim:** The Indian state has treated value inculcation as an institutional design problem for more than sixty years, not as a purely private matter.
**Named evidence:** The Santhanam Committee (1964), quoted at ARC paragraph 2.7.1, asked for "ways and means ... to ensure that idealism and patriotism have the proper place in the ambition of our youth"; the 42nd Amendment (1976) then wrote ten duties into Article 51A, and the 86th Amendment (2002) added Article 21A together with the eleventh duty, 51A(k), on educating a child aged six to fourteen.
**Analysis:** The movement from conduct prohibition to constitutional duty to statutory educational right shows the state progressively relocating value formation from the point of misconduct backwards to the point of upbringing, which is precisely the causal claim the syllabus's second limb asks candidates to defend.
**Qualification:** The relocation is aspirational rather than coercive — Article 51A is expressly non-justiciable with no legal sanction for breach — so the duties operate as a declared public standard and an interpretive aid, and an answer that presents them as enforceable commands misstates the constitutional position.

#### ADMINISTRATIVE APPLICATION IN INDIA

⚠️ A District Education Officer cannot prosecute a parent under Article 51A(k) for keeping a child out of school. What she can do is use the **justiciable** instrument that arrived in the same amendment — Article 21A and the school-entitlement machinery built on it — while using 51A(k) as the normative language of community mobilisation. Naming which instrument is enforceable and which is exhortatory is exactly the precision the examiner rewards.

#### UPSC TRAP

❌ Writing that "Fundamental Duties make value education legally compulsory". → They do not. The constitutional position is that they are non-justiciable, carry no legal sanction, and depend on Parliament choosing to legislate. The enforceable education instrument is Article 21A, added by the same 86th Amendment — a distinction that separates a precise answer from a vague one.

#### RECAP

Conceptual origin: *ethikos*, habit — hence formation by repetition in a community. Institutional chain: 1869–1920 prohibitions → 1930s/1955 Conduct Rules → 1964 Santhanam and CCS Conduct Rules → 1976 42nd Amendment, ten duties → 2002 86th Amendment, Article 21A plus the eleventh duty → 2006 National Colloquium → January 2007 ARC 4th Report → 2020 Mission Karmayogi → 2021 CBC → 6 February 2026 dated figures. Duties are non-justiciable; Article 21A is a fundamental right.
