```text
   THE VERDICT LINE THAT WORKS IN ANY QUESTION IN THIS TOPIC:
   Values decide conduct in the space rules cannot reach; rules decide how
   large that space is. Neither is a substitute; each sets the other's limit.
                                    |
                                    v
   THE THIRD TERM THAT COMPLETES IT: THE EXEMPLAR
   The leader is the mechanism by which a standard becomes CREDIBLE -- a
   demonstration that the standard has been kept under real pressure. That is
   what neither the code nor the sanction can supply.
```

*What the visual shows:* the ARC's own two approaches, their exemplars, the reason each is insufficient stated in the report's own words, and the three-term synthesis that a strong answer states in one line.

#### Core teaching

✅ **The ARC states both approaches and does not simply choose one.** Paragraph 1.4 describes "two, somewhat contrary, approaches". The first is "overemphasis on values and character", whose "implicit assumption is that until values are restored, nothing much can be done to improve the conduct of human beings". The second holds that most people are "fundamentally decent and socially conscious", that a small proportion cannot reconcile individual goals with the good of society, and that "if good behaviour is consistently rewarded and bad behaviour consistently punished, the bulk of the people follow the straight and narrow path."

⚠️ **Why character cannot be dispensed with.** ARC 2.2.4 concedes that rules "cannot provide for all situations" and that discretion widens with seniority. Kautilya's own third proposition concedes that concealed intent is unobservable. Both admissions define a space that no control system enters — and conduct in that space is decided by an internalised standard or by nothing.

⚠️ **Why character alone cannot carry the load.** ARC 1.3 records that corruption is widely regarded as "inevitable" and resistance as "futile". Individual virtue inside a corrosive norm is personally costly and statistically unreliable; a system that depends on exceptional people to function is not a system.

⚠️ **The division of labour, stated precisely.** Values decide conduct in the space rules cannot reach; rules decide how large that space is. Reducing discretionary monopoly, publishing criteria and requiring recorded reasons shrink the unmonitored space; internalised values govern what remains. The two are complements with a boundary, not rivals.

⚠️ **The third term, which most answers omit.** Neither a code nor a sanction can make a standard *credible*. Credibility comes from a demonstration that the standard has been kept by someone real under real pressure — which is the exemplar's specific function, and the reason the syllabus pairs "lessons from the lives of great leaders" with "role of family, society and educational institutions" in one clause. Article 51A(b)'s duty to cherish the ideals of the freedom struggle is the constitutional acknowledgement of that function.

⚠️ **How to deploy the synthesis by question type.** If the question is about *personal conduct* under pressure — a quotation question, a conscience question — lead with the character register and close with the institutional limit. If the question is about *system design* — vigilance, procurement, transfer policy — lead with the design register and close with the discretionary residue that only values fill. If the question is about *inculcation*, use both plus the exemplar term.

⚠️ **The failure mode of each register, which the qualification sentence should name.** Character-first, taken alone, produces moralising that leaves incentives untouched and blames individuals for structural outcomes. Design-first, taken alone, produces surveillance that demoralises honest officers, drives decision-avoidance, and — as Kautilya's own fifth proposition anticipates — forgets to reward integrity at all.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE

> The Second ARC sets out two contrary approaches to corruption and then quietly uses both, because each names the other's limit: values decide conduct in the space rules cannot reach, rules decide how large that space is, and the exemplar is the only mechanism that makes the standard credible enough to be kept when neither is watching.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

`ARC 1.4 — two somewhat contrary approaches` · `overemphasis on values and character` · `consistently rewarded, consistently punished` · `ARC 2.2.4 — rules cannot provide for all situations` · `discretion widens with echelon` · `ARC 1.3 — inevitable and futile` · `values fill the space, rules size the space` · `the exemplar supplies credibility` · `Article 51A(b)` · `moralising vs surveillance as the two failure modes`

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH: claim -> named evidence -> analysis -> qualification

**Claim:** The character-versus-system debate is settled by a division of labour, not by choosing a side.
**Named evidence:** The Second ARC's Fourth Report sets out at paragraph 1.4 both "overemphasis on values and character" and the incentive view that "if good behaviour is consistently rewarded and bad behaviour consistently punished, the bulk of the people follow the straight and narrow path", while conceding at paragraph 2.2.4 that "a system of laws and rules, however elaborate, cannot provide for all situations" and that discretion widens with seniority.
**Analysis:** The two admissions define complementary domains — the incentive system governs observable, rule-covered conduct and can be enlarged by reducing discretionary monopoly, while internalised values govern the residue that remains unobservable however well the system is designed — so the practical reform question is not which approach is right but how much of the residue can be shrunk before values must carry the rest.
**Qualification:** Each register fails characteristically when used alone: character-first reasoning moralises about individuals while leaving the incentives that produced their conduct intact, and design-first reasoning drifts into surveillance that demoralises honest officers and induces decision-avoidance, which is why even Kautilya's account pairs disgorgement with permanence in office for those who serve honestly.

#### ADMINISTRATIVE APPLICATION IN INDIA

⚠️ A department redesigning a licensing counter should do both jobs and say which is which. Shrink the space: publish eligibility criteria, auto-allot files, timestamp every stage, and remove the single-officer monopoly on a decision. Fill the residue: require a recorded reason for every deviation from the published criterion, and make a recorded refusal of improper pressure a positively counted item in the appraisal. The first is Kautilya's work; the second is Gandhi's; the appraisal item is the exemplar function made institutional.

#### UPSC TRAP

❌ Concluding that "ultimately, only values matter, because no system can be foolproof". → The ARC's own text refuses that resolution: it demands sanctions, competent disciplinary bodies and quick action alongside a culture of integrity. The examinable answer states the boundary between the two domains rather than dissolving one into the other.

#### RECAP

ARC 1.4 gives two approaches; the report uses both. Character covers what the system cannot see (2.2.4; Kautilya's proposition 3). System covers what character cannot sustain at scale (1.3). Division of labour: values fill the space, rules size the space. Third term: the exemplar supplies credibility, recognised at Article 51A(b). Failure modes: moralising and surveillance.

### SESSION 19 — The Codified Value Sets: Constitutional, Statutory and Service

**Session 19 of 22 · Stage: Synthesis · Purpose: replace vague "Indian values" with named, citable texts**

#### VISUAL FIRST

```text
   FOUR NAMED VALUE TEXTS A CANDIDATE CAN CITE INSTEAD OF GENERALISING
   ===================================================================

   TEXT 1  THE CONSTITUTION -- addressed to CITIZENS
   -------------------------------------------------------------------------
   Article 51A (Part IVA, inserted by the 42nd Amendment 1976; eleventh duty
   added by the 86th Amendment 2002). ELEVEN duties. Selected:
     (b) cherish and follow the noble ideals that inspired the national
         struggle for freedom                       <- THE EXEMPLAR DUTY
     (e) promote harmony and the spirit of common brotherhood ... transcending
         religious, linguistic and regional or sectional diversities; and
         renounce practices derogatory to the dignity of women
     (h) develop scientific temper, humanism and the spirit of inquiry and
         reform
     (j) strive towards excellence in all spheres of individual and collective
         activity
     (k) provide opportunities for education to his child or ward aged 6-14
   STATUS: NON-JUSTICIABLE. No direct enforcement by courts; no legal sanction
           for violation; Parliament free to enforce by suitable legislation.
   NATURE: "some of them are moral duties while others are civic duties";
           they codify "values which have been a part of the Indian tradition".

   TEXT 2  THE DRAFT PUBLIC SERVICE BILL, 2006 -- addressed to CIVIL SERVANTS
   -------------------------------------------------------------------------
   As recorded at ARC 2.7.5. The salient "values" envisaged:
     * allegiance to the various ideals enshrined in the preamble
     * apolitical functioning
     * good governance for betterment of the people as the primary goal
     * duty to act objectively and impartially
     * accountability and transparency in decision-making
     * maintenance of highest ethical standards
     * merit in selection, consistent with cultural, ethnic and other diversities
     * economy and avoidance of wastage in expenditure
     * healthy and congenial work environment
     * communication, consultation and cooperation in performance of functions
   STATUS: A DRAFT BILL'S LIST as recorded in January 2007. NOT ENACTED LAW.
   ARC ALSO ENVISAGED: a Public Service Code, a Public Service Management Code
           and a Public Service Authority.

   TEXT 3  THE ENFORCEABLE FLOOR -- CCS (CONDUCT) RULES, 1964
   -------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ARC 2.7.5: they contain general norms such as "maintaining integrity and
   absolute devotion to duty" and not indulging in "conduct unbecoming of a
   government servant", but are "generally directed towards cataloguing
   specific activities deemed undesirable".
   ARC'S OWN VERDICT: "There is no Code of Ethics prescribed for civil servants
   in India although such codes exist in other countries ... These Conduct
   Rules do serve a purpose, but they do not constitute a Code of Ethics."

   TEXT 4  THE INTERNATIONAL REFERENCE -- THE NOLAN PRINCIPLES
   -------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ARC 2.2.5 calls the UK Committee on Standards in Public Life (Nolan) output
   "one of the most comprehensive statements of what constitutes ethical
   standards for holders of public office", opening with SELFLESSNESS:
   "Holders of public office should take decisions solely in terms of public
   interest."
   -------------------------------------------------------------------------
   THE EXAM MOVE: never write "Indian values". Write WHICH TEXT, WHOM IT BINDS,
   AND WHETHER IT IS ENFORCEABLE.
```

*What the visual shows:* four citable value texts with four different addressees and four different enforcement statuses. Naming the status is the precision the examiner rewards.

#### Core teaching

✅ **Article 51A, its history and its exact status.** Part IVA and Article 51A were inserted by the 42nd Amendment in 1976, on the Sardar Swaran Singh Committee's recommendation, specifying ten duties; the 86th Amendment in 2002 added the eleventh. The source is explicit that "some of them are moral duties while others are civic duties", that they "refer to such values which have been a part of the Indian tradition, mythology, religions and practices", that they are confined to citizens and do not extend to foreigners, and that "like the Directive Principles, the fundamental duties are also non-justiciable."

✅ **What Parliament did not accept.** The Swaran Singh Committee had also suggested a penalty for non-compliance, a bar on judicial challenge to such a penalty, and a duty to pay taxes. None of the three was incorporated. That single fact is the cleanest available proof that the duties were designed as declared standards rather than enforceable commands.

✅ **The draft Public Service Bill's value list.** ARC 2.7.5 records ten values for civil servants, beginning with "allegiance to the various ideals enshrined in the preamble to the Constitution" and including apolitical functioning, objectivity and impartiality, accountability and transparency in decision-making, merit-based selection consistent with diversity, economy in expenditure, and a congenial work environment. ⚠️ The Commission expressly deferred detailed examination of the Bill to its report on Civil Services Reforms; cite it as a proposed statutory value set.

✅ **The ARC's verdict on the existing enforceable floor.** The Commission finds the CCS (Conduct) Rules, 1964, useful but insufficient: they catalogue proscribed activities and "do not constitute a Code of Ethics", and it records that "there is no Code of Ethics prescribed for civil servants in India". ⚠️ This is the report's January 2007 position and should be dated when quoted.

✅ **The Commission's own recommendation on values.** At paragraph 2.7.9 it takes the view "that there should be a set of Public Service Values which should be stipulated by law", modelled partly on the Australian Public Service Act 1999, where employees must uphold the values, senior executives must promote them, and the Public Service Commissioner evaluates and reports to Parliament on how far agencies have incorporated them. Its recommendation at 2.7.12(a) is that Public Service Values "should be defined and made applicable to all tiers of Government and parastatal organizations", and that "any transgression of these values should be treated as misconduct, inviting punishment."

⚠️ **Note what that recommendation does.** It proposes converting *values* into an enforceable disciplinary standard. That is the precise institutional bridge between this topic's inculcation limb and the sanction machinery of Topics 09 and 16 — and it remains a recommendation, so its status must be stated.

✅ **The Nolan reference.** ARC 2.2.5 describes the UK Nolan Committee's output as "one of the most comprehensive statements of what constitutes ethical standards for holders of public office", and prints its first principle, Selflessness: "Holders of public office should take decisions solely in terms of public interest."

⚠️ **The exam move that this session exists to produce.** Replace "Indian values" with a named text, its addressee and its enforcement status. "Article 51A(e), addressed to citizens, non-justiciable" is a scoring sentence. "Our rich cultural values" is not.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE

> India does not lack written value statements; it has four with four different addressees and four different enforcement statuses — Article 51A binding no one in law, the 2006 draft Bill's ten service values never enacted, the 1964 Conduct Rules which the Second ARC says "do not constitute a Code of Ethics", and the Nolan principles which the Commission itself calls the most comprehensive statement available.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

`Part IVA; 42nd Amendment 1976; Swaran Singh Committee; ten duties` · `86th Amendment 2002; eleventh duty` · `non-justiciable; penalty proposal not accepted` · `moral duties and civic duties; confined to citizens` · `draft Public Service Bill 2006 — ten values; allegiance to the Preamble; apolitical functioning` · `ARC 2.7.9 — values should be stipulated by law; Australian Public Service Act 1999` · `ARC 2.7.12(a) — transgression treated as misconduct` · `CCS (Conduct) Rules 1964 — catalogue, not a Code of Ethics` · `Nolan; Selflessness — solely in terms of public interest`

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH: claim -> named evidence -> analysis -> qualification

**Claim:** India's civil-service value problem is one of enforceability and specification, not of absent statements.
**Named evidence:** The Second ARC records at paragraph 2.7.5 that the CCS (Conduct) Rules "do serve a purpose, but they do not constitute a Code of Ethics" and that "there is no Code of Ethics prescribed for civil servants in India", and it therefore recommends at paragraphs 2.7.9 and 2.7.12(a) that Public Service Values "should be stipulated by law", made applicable to all tiers of government and parastatal organisations, with "any transgression of these values ... treated as misconduct, inviting punishment."
**Analysis:** The gap the Commission identifies is structural rather than rhetorical: conduct rules enumerate prohibited acts and therefore fail exactly where conduct is novel or where the objectionable element is an attitude rather than an act, whereas a stipulated value set with disciplinary force would reach the conduct that lies between full legality and clear misconduct.
**Qualification:** The proposal remains a recommendation of January 2007 accompanied by a draft Bill whose detailed examination the Commission itself deferred, so it must be cited as a recommendation rather than as law, and its own risk — that vaguely worded values could support arbitrary disciplinary action — has to be conceded alongside its promise.

#### ADMINISTRATIVE APPLICATION IN INDIA

⚠️ A head of department asked to "instil values" in a wing should not draft a fresh charter. Three of the four texts already exist and bind or address her staff. The useful act is to take two items from the 2006 list — objectivity and impartiality, and accountability and transparency in decision-making — and specify what each requires in that wing's actual work: which decisions must carry recorded reasons, which relationships trigger recusal, and which registers are published. A value that has not been converted into a named procedural obligation has not been instilled.

#### UPSC TRAP

❌ Writing that the Fundamental Duties "impose an obligation on citizens to uphold values" without stating their status. → They are non-justiciable, carry no legal sanction, and the penalty clause the Swaran Singh Committee proposed was expressly not incorporated. ❌ Equally, describing the CCS (Conduct) Rules as India's code of ethics. The ARC's own finding is the opposite.

#### RECAP

Four texts, four addressees, four statuses. Article 51A: citizens, non-justiciable, eleven duties, 1976 plus 2002, penalty proposal rejected. Draft Public Service Bill 2006: civil servants, ten values, not enacted, plus a proposed Public Service Code, Management Code and Authority. CCS (Conduct) Rules 1964: enforceable but a catalogue, not a Code of Ethics. Nolan: the ARC's chosen international reference, Selflessness first. ARC 2.7.9 and 2.7.12(a) recommend values stipulated by law with transgression treated as misconduct.

### SESSION 20 — Institutional Application: Making Value Education Do Administrative Work

**Session 20 of 22 · Stage: Synthesis · Purpose: convert the whole topic into one operating chain**

#### VISUAL FIRST

```text
   THE VALUE-EDUCATION CHAIN, AND THE THREE PLACES IT BREAKS
   ========================================================

   [1] SOURCE          family, society, religion, peer group, school
        |                (Sessions 5-7)
        v
   [2] SELECTION       recruitment tests aptitude and knowledge;
        |              it CANNOT test internalisation (Session 8)
        |              BREAK POINT A: we select for what is observable
        v
   [3] FORMATION       foundation course, in-service training,
        |              Mission Karmayogi FRAC competencies, iGOT modules
        |              BREAK POINT B: instruction is Mechanism 4 -- the
        |              weakest alone; ARC 2.7.2 says the attitudes are
        |              "not skills which could be easily imbibed after
        |              joining the civil services"
        v
   [4] REINFORCEMENT   appraisal, posting, promotion, recognition
        |              BREAK POINT C: the ESPOUSED value and the REWARDED
        |              conduct diverge (Session 3, Cut 4). This is the
        |              decisive break, because it TEACHES a value -- just
        |              not the printed one.
        v
   [5] SANCTION        disciplinary machinery; ARC 1.1 requires bodies that
        |              investigate AND "impose sanctions quickly"
        v
   [6] CONDUCT UNDER DISCRETION  <- the only place any of this is tested

   ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   MISSION KARMAYOGI AGAINST THE CHAIN  (the honest assessment)
   ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   WHAT IT CAN DO                       WHAT MUST NOT BE CLAIMED FROM IT
   * make competencies EXPLICIT (FRAC)  * that completions prove internalisation
   * make learning CONTINUOUS, not      * a general APAR linkage, absent a
     one-off at induction                 service-specific DoPT order naming scope
   * make it ROLE-SPECIFIC rather       * that reach figures are a programme total
     than generic                         rather than the 6 Feb 2026 position
   * reach scale: 1.49 cr users,        * that training substitutes for the
     4,342 courses, 7.26 cr course        appraisal redesign at BREAK POINT C
     completions, 23 languages
     (dated 6 February 2026)
   ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   THE VERDICT: Mission Karmayogi is a strong intervention at STEP 3 and a
   weak one at STEP 4 unless what is rewarded changes too.
```

*What the visual shows:* the full chain from source to conduct, its three break points, and a bounded assessment of the current instrument against them.

#### Core teaching

📰 **The instrument.** Mission Karmayogi, the National Programme for Civil Services Capacity Building, was approved by the Union Cabinet on **2 September 2020**. It builds behavioural competencies — values, attitude and integrity — through the **FRAC** approach and the **iGOT-Karmayogi** platform. The **Capacity Building Commission** was established on **1 April 2021**. Figures reported to Parliament with data as on **6 February 2026**: over 1.49 crore users, 4,342 courses, 7.26 crore course completions and 23 languages.

⚠️ **Break Point A: selection.** Recruitment can test aptitude, knowledge and, through the interview, expressed judgement. It cannot test what a candidate does when unobserved. This is not a defect in the examination; it is the observability problem of Session 8. The honest consequence is that the service must assume it has recruited a normal distribution of dispositions and design accordingly — which is Kautilya's premise stated without cynicism.

⚠️ **Break Point B: formation by instruction alone.** ARC 2.7.2 is the governing statement: the capacity for "subordination of the self to a larger, societal good" and "a spirit of empathy for those in need of ameliorative state interventions" comprises attitudes that "are not skills which could be easily imbibed after joining the civil services". Training can activate, refine, name and hold accountable. It is weak at manufacturing.

⚠️ **Break Point C is the decisive one.** If the appraisal counts disposal volume and never counts a recorded refusal of improper pressure, then the organisation is teaching throughput — accurately and continuously, by Mechanism 2. No training budget defeats an appraisal form. This is why an answer that responds to "how do we build values in the service" with training proposals alone is incomplete.

⚠️ **What can actually be changed at Break Point C.** Four items, all within existing administrative power: make recorded reasons a graded item rather than a formality; create a recorded route for reporting improper pressure and count its use positively rather than as friction; publish minimum-tenure norms so that stability visibly rewards integrity — Kautilya's fifth proposition, institutionalised; and make one senior officer's recusal visible each year, because modelling is Mechanism 1 and nothing else transmits as fast.

✅ **The sanction step, in the report's own words.** ARC 1.1 requires "competent disciplinary bodies to investigate allegations of violations and impose sanctions quickly". Speed is part of the standard, not an administrative nicety, because a sanction that arrives after the officer's retirement teaches nothing.

⚠️ **The bounded verdict on the current instrument.** Mission Karmayogi is a strong intervention at Step 3 — explicit competencies, continuous rather than one-off learning, role-specific content and genuine scale — and a weak one at Step 4 unless what is rewarded changes with it. Say exactly that; it is both defensible and analytically sharper than either praise or dismissal.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE

> A value-education programme is only as strong as the appraisal that follows it: Mission Karmayogi's dated figures of 6 February 2026 — 1.49 crore users and 7.26 crore course completions — establish reach at the formation step, but a service whose appraisal counts disposal and never counts a recorded refusal is still teaching throughput, continuously and effectively.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

`source, selection, formation, reinforcement, sanction, conduct under discretion` · `Mission Karmayogi, 2 September 2020; FRAC; iGOT-Karmayogi` · `Capacity Building Commission, 1 April 2021` · `1.49 crore users, 4,342 courses, 7.26 crore completions, 23 languages (6 February 2026)` · `ARC 2.7.2 — not easily imbibed after joining` · `espoused-enacted divergence at the appraisal` · `recorded reasons as a graded item` · `published minimum-tenure norms` · `ARC 1.1 — impose sanctions quickly` · `reach is not internalisation`

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH: claim -> named evidence -> analysis -> qualification

**Claim:** Mission Karmayogi strengthens the formation step of India's value chain but cannot by itself repair the reinforcement step.
**Named evidence:** The programme was approved by the Union Cabinet on 2 September 2020 and builds behavioural competencies through the FRAC framework and the iGOT-Karmayogi platform, with the Capacity Building Commission established on 1 April 2021 and reported figures as on 6 February 2026 of over 1.49 crore users, 4,342 courses, 7.26 crore course completions and 23 languages; the Second ARC had already warned at paragraph 2.7.2 that such attitudes "are not skills which could be easily imbibed after joining the civil services."
**Analysis:** Explicit, role-specific and continuous learning addresses the weakness of one-off induction training and gives the service a shared vocabulary for behavioural competence, but conduct is repeated because it is rewarded, so an appraisal that counts disposal volume while ignoring recorded refusals of improper pressure will continue to transmit throughput as the operative value whatever the training catalogue contains.
**Qualification:** The reported figures measure reach rather than internalisation, they are the position on a stated date rather than a programme total, and no general APAR linkage should be asserted without a service-specific DoPT order naming its scope — so the programme's claim must be bounded to what course completions can actually evidence.

#### ADMINISTRATIVE APPLICATION IN INDIA

⚠️ A cadre-controlling authority that wants the training investment to hold has one high-leverage move available without new legislation: add a single graded item to the annual appraisal for "quality of recorded reasoning in discretionary decisions", audited on a sample. It costs nothing, it is observable, it rewards exactly the conduct the training teaches, and it closes Break Point C. Everything else in the chain is downstream of what the appraisal counts.

#### UPSC TRAP

❌ Citing Mission Karmayogi's user and completion figures as evidence that civil-service ethics has improved. → They evidence reach. The package's own limit statement — that the figures "demonstrate reach and completions, not ethical internalisation" — is the sentence that earns the mark. ❌ Equally, asserting an APAR linkage without naming the order that creates it.

#### RECAP

Chain: source → selection → formation → reinforcement → sanction → conduct under discretion. Break A: selection cannot test internalisation. Break B: instruction alone is weak (ARC 2.7.2). Break C, decisive: espoused value diverges from rewarded conduct. Mission Karmayogi (2 September 2020; CBC 1 April 2021; 6 February 2026 figures) is strong at Step 3, weak at Step 4 unless appraisal changes. Sanctions must be quick (ARC 1.1).

---

### SESSION 21 — Criticism and Qualification: Where Exemplar Ethics Goes Wrong

**Session 21 of 22 · Stage: Advanced application · Purpose: supply the counter-position every high answer needs**

#### VISUAL FIRST

```text
   SIX FAILURE MODES OF EXEMPLAR-BASED ETHICS -- AND THE CORRECTIVE FOR EACH
   ========================================================================

   1 THE QUOTATION SCRAPBOOK
     symptom: an answer that is a chain of sayings with no rule between them
     corrective: FIVE-SLOT TEST (Session 10). Delete any quotation that could
                 be removed without weakening the argument.
   ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   2 SELECTIVE QUOTATION
     symptom: the same leader is cited to justify opposite choices
     corrective: name the SPECIFIC INSTITUTIONAL CONTEXT the teaching addressed
                 -- an unbounded teaching is infinitely flexible
   ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   3 ANACHRONISM
     symptom: "Kautilya recommended a vigilance commission"
     corrective: MINIMUM DATING RULE. Classical sources supply DIAGNOSES and
                 INCENTIVE LOGIC, not institutional designs
   ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   4 HERO-WORSHIP / INFALLIBILITY
     symptom: admired character treated as a warrant for any inferred position
     corrective: a life is evidence about what is POSSIBLE, not proof about
                 what is CORRECT. State one limitation per exemplar
   ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   5 MORAL SUBSTITUTION ("value talk as evasion")
     symptom: "we need a change of values" REPLACES naming the incentive,
              the discretion and the accountability gap
     corrective: ARC 1.4's warning against "overemphasis on values and
                 character"; every value claim must terminate in a mechanism
   ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   6 FABRICATED PROVENANCE
     symptom: inventing a source for an untraceable line
     corrective: the PROVENANCE LADDER. "Widely attributed" costs three words
                 and protects the whole answer
   ===========================================================================
   AND THE COUNTER-CRITICISM, WHICH MUST ALSO BE MADE:
   dismissing exemplars entirely is its own error. Codes and sanctions cannot
   make a standard CREDIBLE or MEMORABLE; only a kept example can. Article
   51A(b) makes cherishing the ideals of the freedom struggle a citizen duty.
```

*What the visual shows:* six failure modes with six correctives, plus the counter-criticism that prevents the session from becoming mere debunking.

#### Core teaching

⚠️ **Failure mode 1, the quotation scrapbook.** The characteristic weak answer in this topic is a sequence of admired sayings with no administrative rule between them. The test is deletion: if a quotation can be removed without weakening the argument, it was ornamental and its words would have scored more as analysis.

⚠️ **Failure mode 2, selective quotation.** Most substantial corpora contain material supporting opposite administrative choices. Gandhi can be quoted for both radical decentralisation and for disciplined obedience within a movement; Kautilya for both suspicion of officers and for rewarding them with permanence. The corrective is to name the institutional context the teaching addressed, because an unbounded teaching is infinitely flexible and therefore proves nothing.

⚠️ **Failure mode 3, anachronism.** A classical or colonial-era teaching addressed a different institutional world with no concept of judicial review, a free press or citizen rights. Use such sources for diagnosis and incentive logic; do not attribute institutions to them.

⚠️ **Failure mode 4, hero-worship.** Admired character is not infallibility, and an inferred position is not a stated one. Each exemplar in this package therefore carries an explicit limitation, and an answer should carry at least one.

⚠️ **Failure mode 5, moral substitution — the most consequential.** "We need a change in values" can *replace* the analysis rather than support it. The ARC names this danger directly at paragraph 1.4, describing the first approach as an "overemphasis on values and character" whose "implicit assumption is that until values are restored, nothing much can be done to improve the conduct of human beings" — a position that is, in effect, an argument for doing nothing institutional. Every value claim in an answer must terminate in a mechanism.

⚠️ **Failure mode 6, fabricated provenance.** The single unrecoverable error. Three words — "widely attributed to" — cost nothing and protect the whole answer.

⚠️ **The counter-criticism, which prevents this session from becoming debunking.** Exemplars do work nothing else does. They make a standard **memorable** under time pressure, **credible** by demonstrating that it has been kept, and **imaginable** beyond the current rulebook's contemplation. Article 51A(b) writes that function into the Constitution as a citizen duty to cherish the ideals of the national struggle.

⚠️ **A specific Indian caution about exemplar politics.** Public invocation of a leader's name is itself a contested political act, and different constituencies claim the same figure for incompatible programmes. A civil servant's safeguard is to translate the teaching into a **procedural** obligation — a published criterion, a recorded reason, a declared recusal — because a procedure can be reviewed on its own terms whereas an appeal to a leader's authority cannot.

⚠️ **The honest limit on the whole topic.** No amount of exemplar study predicts conduct. The relationship between studying an admirable life and behaving admirably under pressure is not established by anything in the sources used here, and an answer that claims it is has overstated. What can be defended is narrower and sufficient: exemplars supply portable standards, credibility and moral imagination, and those are inputs to conduct rather than guarantees of it.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE

> The gravest failure of exemplar ethics is not inaccuracy but substitution — the Second ARC itself warns against an "overemphasis on values and character" whose implicit assumption is that "until values are restored, nothing much can be done" — so every teaching cited in a GS-IV answer must terminate in a named mechanism rather than in admiration.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

`quotation scrapbook; the deletion test` · `selective quotation; name the institutional context` · `anachronism; diagnosis not design` · `hero-worship; possible is not correct` · `moral substitution; ARC 1.4 overemphasis on values and character` · `fabricated provenance; "widely attributed"` · `exemplars supply memorability, credibility, moral imagination` · `Article 51A(b)` · `translate into a procedural obligation, not an appeal to authority`

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH: claim -> named evidence -> analysis -> qualification

**Claim:** Invoking values can obstruct reform as easily as it can motivate it.
**Named evidence:** The Second ARC identifies at paragraph 1.4 an approach it calls "overemphasis on values and character", in which "many people lament the decline in values and the consequent rise in corruption", and whose "implicit assumption is that until values are restored, nothing much can be done to improve the conduct of human beings."
**Analysis:** So framed, a values discourse becomes a counsel of postponement — it locates the cause in a diffuse cultural condition that no administrator can alter within a tenure, and thereby excuses the concrete work of reducing discretionary monopoly, publishing criteria, requiring recorded reasons and disposing of disciplinary cases quickly.
**Qualification:** The corrective is not to abandon values language but to bound it, since the same report demands "a robust culture of integrity" alongside sanctions; the workable rule is that every value claim must terminate in a named mechanism, and that an exemplar earns its place in an answer only when it changes what a specific officer is required to do.

#### ADMINISTRATIVE APPLICATION IN INDIA

⚠️ A review meeting on scheme leakage that concludes with an appeal to officers' conscience has performed failure mode 5. The same meeting is salvaged by one question: which discretion produced the leakage, who exercised it, and what recorded criterion would have removed it? The value language then has somewhere to land — the criterion is published, and integrity is what the officer exercises inside a smaller and better-lit space.

#### UPSC TRAP

❌ Writing a critical-examination answer that only celebrates exemplars, or only debunks them. → The directive requires both sides. The scoring structure is: state what exemplars uniquely supply (memorability, credibility, moral imagination, Article 51A(b)), then state the six failure modes, then give a bounded verdict — exemplars are inputs to conduct, not guarantees of it, and must terminate in mechanisms.

#### RECAP

Six failure modes: scrapbook, selective quotation, anachronism, hero-worship, moral substitution (ARC 1.4), fabricated provenance. Six correctives: five-slot test, name the context, minimum dating, state a limitation, terminate in a mechanism, use the provenance ladder. Counter-criticism: exemplars supply memorability, credibility and moral imagination, recognised at Article 51A(b). Honest limit: exemplar study does not predict conduct.

---

### SESSION 22 — Writing the GS-IV Quotation and Human-Values Answer

**Session 22 of 22 · Stage: Advanced application · Purpose: convert everything above into marks**

#### VISUAL FIRST

```text
   THE VERIFIED GS-IV MARKS CONTRACT  (read directly from both local papers)
   ========================================================================
   SECTION A : every sub-question -> 10 marks -> "(Answer in 150 words)"
   SECTION B : every case study    -> 20 marks -> "(Answer in 250 words)"
   GS-IV SETS NO 15-MARK QUESTION.
   ------------------------------------------------------------------------

   ARCHITECTURE 1 -- THE QUOTATION ANSWER (10 marks / 150 words)
   "What does it convey to you in the present context?"
   +----------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | 1 RESTATE the idea in ONE line, in your own words              ~20 w  |
   | 2 CONTEXT of the thinker in ONE line (+ attribution caution     ~20 w |
   |   in ONE CLAUSE if the provenance is P3/P4)                           |
   | 3 NAMED ADMINISTRATIVE MECHANISM -- the rule that follows      ~45 w  |
   | 4 ONE CONCRETE INDIAN EXAMPLE doing analytical work            ~40 w  |
   | 5 ONE-LINE LIMIT + normative takeaway                          ~25 w  |
   +----------------------------------------------------------------------+
   WHAT LOSES MARKS: biography; a second quotation; "in conclusion, values
   are very important for a civil servant."

   ARCHITECTURE 2 -- THE INCULCATION ANSWER (10 marks / 150 words)
   "role of family / society / educational institutions"
   +----------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | 1 DEFINE the transmission problem (value != rule)               ~20 w |
   | 2 NAME THE MECHANISM for each institution asked about          ~55 w  |
   |   (modelling / reinforcement / identification / instruction /         |
   |    reflective revision -- Session 4)                                  |
   | 3 ONE NAMED TEXT OR FINDING per institution                    ~40 w  |
   |   family -> ARC's disgrace incentive, Art. 51A(k)                     |
   |   society -> Cooter's everyday-norms finding                          |
   |   school -> Art. 21A, Art. 51A(b)/(h), ARC 2.7.2                      |
   | 4 THE FAILURE MODE of each (they reproduce as well as correct) ~20 w  |
   | 5 QUALIFIED VERDICT with the generational horizon              ~15 w  |
   +----------------------------------------------------------------------+

   ARCHITECTURE 3 -- THE CASE STUDY (20 marks / 250 words) [Topic 22 owns this]
   FACTS/ASSUMPTIONS -> STAKEHOLDERS -> VALUES IN CONFLICT -> AT LEAST THREE
   OPTIONS with CONSEQUENCES -> LAWFUL ETHICAL DECISION -> IMPLEMENTATION ->
   SAFEGUARD AND RESIDUAL RISK.
   THIS TOPIC'S CONTRIBUTION: naming the values precisely, ordering them by
   the lexical filter (Session 9), and stating what the decision costs.

   ------------------------------------------------------------------------
   THE SELECTABLE EVIDENCE POOL -- PICK 1-2, NEVER EXHAUST IT
   ------------------------------------------------------------------------
   E1 Values are transmitted, not innate | Gandhi's Seven Social Sins, ARC Box
      2.8 | gives a decoupling checklist | LIMIT: the leisure/pleasure variance
      shows even canonical lists have transmission gaps
   E2 Selective learning beats imitation | Vivekananda, 1897 Lahore | supports
      adapting global models | LIMIT: over-applied, it resists legitimate reform
   E3 Institution-building corrects structural inequality | Bhide Wada, 1848 |
      models durable correction | LIMIT: one school did not resolve the system
   E4 Personal integrity scales into order | Kalam's chain (credited to Sathya
      Sai Baba); ARC's Confucian Pole star | LIMIT: rhetorical, not demonstrated
   E5 Equanimity is administratively valuable | Tirukkural 623 | maps to crisis
      composure | LIMIT: equanimity is not passivity
   E6 Corruption is structural | Kautilya's five propositions, ARC Annexure-I(2)
      | grounds incentive design | LIMIT: alone it licenses harmful surveillance
   E7 Values must be inculcated early | ARC 2.7.2; Santhanam 1964 | grounds the
      school claim | LIMIT: the ethos takes successive generations
   ------------------------------------------------------------------------
   COUNTERPOINT TEMPLATE: "This teaching is administratively valuable because
   [named mechanism], but its limitation is [X]; it should therefore be applied
   alongside [named institutional safeguard], not relied on alone."
```

*What the visual shows:* the verified marks contract, three answer architectures with word budgets, a seven-item evidence pool with limitations attached, and a counterpoint template.

#### Core teaching

✅ **The marks contract is verified, not assumed.** Both locally held GS-IV papers print "(Answer in 150 words)" against every 10-mark Section-A sub-question and "(Answer in 250 words)" against every 20-mark Section-B case study. **GS-IV sets no 15-mark question.** Any 15-mark item in the practice set below is therefore labelled *extended analytical practice*, not a paper format.

⚠️ **Directive decoding for this topic.** "What does it convey to you in the present context" tests application, not memory: restate, contextualise, name the mechanism, illustrate, qualify. "Discuss" and "Reflect" require balanced multi-angle treatment with one limitation. "Examine" requires a position plus its limits. "Explain" or "Illustrate" requires a precise mechanism with one example doing the analytical work. "Justify" requires the claim to be defended with examples rather than merely described.

⚠️ **Word budgeting is the difference between a 5 and a 7 in a 150-word answer.** The quotation architecture above allocates roughly 20, 20, 45, 40 and 25 words. The largest block is the administrative mechanism, because that is what is being tested; biography receives none.

⚠️ **The attribution caution goes in a clause, never a paragraph.** "As the paper sets it" or "widely attributed" is enough. Spending forty words auditing a citation in a 150-word answer answers a question that was not asked.

⚠️ **For inculcation questions, name the mechanism rather than listing institutions.** Every candidate will write "family, society and school play an important role". The mark separates on *how*: modelling, reinforcement, identification, instruction, reflective revision — with a named text or finding attached to each institution and its failure mode conceded.

⚠️ **The three-part closing move.** Every answer in this topic should end with a bounded verdict, not a slogan. The template is: valuable because [mechanism], limited because [X], therefore apply alongside [safeguard]. That single sentence supplies the qualification the marking scheme rewards and prevents the answer from ending on "values are important".

⚠️ **Where this topic hands off.** Attitude change is Topic 03; foundational civil-service values and aptitude are Topic 04; emotional intelligence and crisis composure mechanics are Topic 05; thinker-level doctrinal depth is Topics 06 and 07; public-service values and dilemmas are Topic 09; law-versus-conscience as a source of guidance is Topic 10; probity is Topic 14; codes are Topic 16; vigilance and the protection of honest officers is Topic 21; and the case-study architecture is Topic 22. Naming the boundary is itself a mark of precision.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE

> In a 150-word quotation answer the marks are distributed almost entirely to one block — the named administrative mechanism the teaching produces — so the discipline is to spend twenty words restating, twenty on context and caution, forty-five on the mechanism, forty on one Indian example and twenty-five on the limit, and none at all on biography.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

`Section A: 10 marks, 150 words; Section B: 20 marks, 250 words; no 15-mark question` · `restate, context, mechanism, example, limit` · `attribution caution in a clause` · `name the transmission mechanism, not the institution` · `valuable because / limited because / apply alongside` · `selectable evidence pool E1–E7` · `hand-offs: Topics 03, 04, 05, 06, 07, 09, 10, 14, 16, 21, 22`

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH: claim -> named evidence -> analysis -> qualification

**Claim:** A quotation answer earns its marks in the translation block, not in the exposition.
**Named evidence:** Both the 2024 and 2025 GS-IV papers set their quotation questions in the identical form — "Given below are three quotations of great thinkers. What do each of these quotations convey to you in the present context?" — with "(Answer in 150 words)" and 10 marks against each sub-part.
**Analysis:** The directive asks what the quotation conveys *to the candidate* *in the present context*, which makes the examinable object the derived administrative rule and its contemporary application, so the exposition of the thinker's system is not merely unnecessary but actively costly at 150 words.
**Qualification:** Compression must not become assertion — a rule stated without an example is unevidenced and a limitation omitted reads as naivety — so the correct economy is one mechanism, one named Indian illustration and one bounded limit, rather than several undeveloped points.

#### ADMINISTRATIVE APPLICATION IN INDIA

⚠️ The same discipline governs a real note to government. A proposal that opens with principle, names the specific procedural change, cites one comparable Indian instance, and states what the change will not fix is approved faster than one that argues at length from first principles. The GS-IV answer format is not an artificial exam convention; it is the compressed form of an administrative recommendation.

#### UPSC TRAP

❌ Writing a 15-mark GS-IV answer because a coaching source says so. → Both locally held official papers set only 10-mark Section-A sub-questions at 150 words and 20-mark Section-B case studies at 250 words. Practising at 15 marks is useful for depth, but the format must be labelled as practice, and an answer written to a 15-mark structure in the examination will be mis-budgeted.

#### RECAP

Verified contract: 10 marks / 150 words in Section A, 20 marks / 250 words in Section B, no 15-mark question. Three architectures: quotation (restate, context, mechanism, example, limit), inculcation (mechanism per institution with a named text and a conceded failure mode), case study (Topic 22's architecture, this topic's value content). Evidence pool E1–E7 with limitations. Closing move: valuable because, limited because, apply alongside.

---

### ETHICS DEEP-REVIEW CORE CONTROL

- **Must remember:** Human values are enduring, trans-situational standards formed through five mechanisms — modelling, reinforcement, identification, instruction and reflective revision — operating in the family, in society and in educational institutions; lessons from Gandhi, Vivekananda, Kautilya, Mahavir, Ambedkar, Savitribai Phule, Patel, Kalam, Thiruvalluvar and others require the value, the action or text, the context, a named administrative rule and a limitation, never an ornamental quotation.
- **Close distinction:** anecdote is not doctrine; admired character is not infallibility; anekantavada is epistemic humility and not moral relativism; nishkama karma is non-attachment to credit and not indifference to outcomes; trusteeship is not a substitute for enforceable justice; Article 51A is non-justiciable while Article 21A is a fundamental right; conduct rules are a catalogue and not a Code of Ethics; and reach figures are not evidence of internalisation.
- **Evidence / authority / application limit:** use named India-centric episodes only where provenance is secure; label the four provenance states honestly — primary text read, official report's attribution, UPSC's printed attribution, and widely circulated without a traceable source; and connect every value to an institutional or behavioural mechanism rather than to admiration.
## BASIC MCQS / REMEDIATION

These 32 original drills precede the PYQ section. Twenty-four are core drills across the complete topic; eight are remedial drills aimed at the specific errors this topic reliably produces. Every option carries its own explanation, and every question carries a question-specific trap note.

### CORE DRILLS 1–24

#### MCQ 1

A trainee argues that because she has always disliked bribery, honesty is one of her values. Which statement identifies the feature that would actually establish honesty as a **value** rather than as something weaker?

- **A.** That the standard is enduring and operates across unrelated situations, rather than attaching to a single object, occasion or person.
- **B.** That she experiences a strong negative emotion when the subject of bribery is raised in conversation or in a training discussion.
- **C.** That she can define bribery accurately and can name the statutory provision under which it is punishable in India.
- **D.** That her department has notified a conduct rule prohibiting the acceptance of illegal gratification by its officers.

**Answer:** A

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — correct.** A value is defined by two properties, endurance and trans-situationality. Only a standard that persists over time and governs choice across unrelated situations qualifies; that is precisely what distinguishes it from a mood or an attitude.
- **B — wrong.** A strong feeling about one object is the signature of an attitude, not a value. Feeling is at the base of the value ladder and is the least stable level; it can coexist with dishonest conduct in an unrelated file.
- **C — wrong.** Accurate definitional knowledge is cognitive content. Knowing what bribery is and which provision punishes it establishes information, and information has never been sufficient for conduct.
- **D — wrong.** A notified conduct rule is an external, enforceable standard sitting above the value on the ladder. It describes what the organisation requires, not what the individual holds.

> **Trap:** The stem offers an emotional report ("she has always disliked") and invites the candidate to accept it as evidence of a value. The examinable test is structural — endurance plus trans-situationality — not the intensity of the feeling reported.

---

#### MCQ 2

Consider the following two statements.

1. A value is object-specific, whereas an attitude governs choice across unrelated situations.
2. A trainee may hold a favourable attitude towards a cleanliness campaign and still falsify an attendance register.

Which of the statements is/are correct?

- **A.** 1 only
- **B.** 2 only
- **C.** Both 1 and 2
- **D.** Neither 1 nor 2

**Answer:** B

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — wrong.** Statement 1 reverses the two terms. It is the *attitude* that is object-specific and the *value* that is trans-situational, so accepting statement 1 endorses the standard confusion this topic exists to correct.
- **B — correct.** Statement 1 is reversed and therefore false, while statement 2 is exactly the diagnostic illustration: a favourable evaluation attached to one object leaves conduct in an unrelated situation untouched, which is what "object-specific" means in practice.
- **C — wrong.** This accepts the reversal in statement 1. Both statements cannot be correct because statement 2's illustration only makes sense on the corrected definitions, where the attitude is the narrow item.
- **D — wrong.** Statement 2 is a straightforward and correct application of the value–attitude distinction, so rejecting both statements discards a true one.

> **Trap:** Two-statement items in this area routinely swap "value" and "attitude" in one clause. Read the definitional statement first and check the direction of the distinction before evaluating the illustration.

---

#### MCQ 3

The Second ARC's Fourth Report describes the relationship between ethical values and the legal system. Which statement most accurately reproduces the Commission's position?

- **A.** Values and law are alternative and competing instruments of social control, so a society that succeeds in strengthening one of them will necessarily find that its reliance on the other declines in proportion.
- **B.** Law is prior to values, because citizens acquire their sense of right and wrong only after the legislature has prescribed particular conduct and attached penalties to its breach, so that the statute book is the origin rather than the expression of a shared moral position.
- **C.** Ethics provides the basis for the creation of laws and rules, because it is the moral ideas of people that give rise to and shape their character, and the legal system emanates from a shared vision of what is good and just.
- **D.** Values operate only in the private sphere of individual conscience, so the public legal order neither draws upon them when framing its rules nor is affected in any measurable way by their decline among the population it governs.

**Answer:** C

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — wrong.** The report treats the two as sequentially related rather than as competing substitutes. It uses values as the source of law and then demands enforcement machinery, which is a complementary and not a rival account.
- **B — wrong.** This inverts paragraph 2.2.1. The Commission places moral ideas upstream of legislation, not downstream of it, and explicitly derives the character of laws from a pre-existing shared vision.
- **C — correct.** Paragraph 2.2.1 states that ethics "provides the basis for the creation of such laws and rules", that "it is the moral ideas of people that give rise to and shapes the character of laws and rules", and that "our legal system emanates from a shared vision of what is good and just."
- **D — wrong.** The report's entire subject is ethics in *public* life, opening from the premise that every holder of public office is accountable to the people, so confining values to the private sphere contradicts the chapter's first sentence.

> **Trap:** The plausible-sounding inversion in option B mirrors a common examination framing in which law is treated as the origin of morality. The report's direction of causation runs the other way, and the phrase "emanates from a shared vision" is the marker.

---

#### MCQ 4

Which of the following best captures why the Second ARC's Fourth Report insists that values remain indispensable **despite** an elaborate framework of rules?

- **A.** Because Indian conduct rules were drafted before independence and have not since been amended to cover contemporary forms of misconduct, leaving the disciplinary authority without any applicable norm in most modern situations.
- **B.** Because a comprehensive code of ethics for civil servants has now been enacted and has rendered the older conduct rules formally redundant, so that values survive only as a historical explanation of provisions that are already law.
- **C.** Because the Commission regards enforcement as undesirable and prefers moral persuasion to disciplinary machinery in all cases, treating investigation and sanction as instruments that damage the culture they are meant to protect.
- **D.** Because a system of laws and rules, however elaborate, cannot provide for all situations, and the ambit of discretion widens as one rises in the service, so that "one cannot mandate honesty".

**Answer:** D

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — wrong.** The report records a continuing history of amendment: the compendium became distinct rules in 1955, the Santhanam Committee produced the enlarged 1964 version, and later additions covered courtesy, dowry, sexual harassment and child domestic labour.
- **B — wrong.** The Commission's finding is the opposite. It states that "there is no Code of Ethics prescribed for civil servants in India" and that the conduct rules "do not constitute a Code of Ethics".
- **C — wrong.** The report demands sanctions against violations and "competent disciplinary bodies to investigate allegations of violations and impose sanctions quickly". It does not prefer persuasion to enforcement; it requires both.
- **D — correct.** Paragraph 2.2.4 states that rules "cannot provide for all situations" and that "the higher the echelon in public service, the greater is the ambit of discretion", while the Preface adds that "one cannot mandate honesty."

> **Trap:** Options A and B are factual claims about Indian conduct rules that the report itself contradicts. The question is testing whether the candidate knows the *structural* argument — the discretionary residue — rather than a grievance about drafting.

---

#### MCQ 5

The Second ARC quotes an earlier official committee which asked that "idealism and patriotism have the proper place in the ambition of our youth" and identified "the lack of moral earnestness" as perhaps the greatest single factor hampering strong traditions of integrity and efficiency. Which committee is this, and in which year did it report?

- **A.** The Committee on Prevention of Corruption, known as the Santhanam Committee, in 1964.
- **B.** The Sardar Swaran Singh Committee, in 1976.
- **C.** The National Commission to Review the Working of the Constitution, in 2002.
- **D.** The Committee on Standards in Public Life chaired by Lord Nolan, in 1995.

**Answer:** A

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — correct.** The passage is quoted at ARC paragraph 2.7.1 and attributed there to the "Committee on Prevention of Corruption ('Santhanam Committee'-1964)". The same committee's recommendations produced the enlarged CCS (Conduct) Rules of 1964.
- **B — wrong.** The Swaran Singh Committee was constituted in 1976 to recommend Fundamental Duties. It suggested eight duties, of which the 42nd Amendment enacted ten, and it dealt with citizen duties rather than with the moral earnestness of youth in the context of corruption.
- **C — wrong.** The NCRWC appears in the report on a different subject entirely, in connection with special election benches in the High Courts for the disposal of election petitions.
- **D — wrong.** The Nolan Committee appears at paragraph 2.2.5 as the source of the seven principles of public life, which are standards for holders of public office rather than an Indian statement about youth and moral earnestness.

> **Trap:** All four bodies genuinely appear in this area of the syllabus, and three of them appear in this very report. The discriminating detail is the subject matter — youth, ambition and moral earnestness in the context of corruption prevention — which is Santhanam's, not Nolan's or Swaran Singh's.

---

#### MCQ 6

Which statement correctly describes the constitutional insertion of the Fundamental Duties?

- **A.** Article 51A was part of the Constitution as adopted in 1950 and was merely renumbered by a later amendment, the framers having considered citizen duties to be as necessary as the fundamental rights placed in Part III.
- **B.** Part IVA containing Article 51A was inserted by the 42nd Constitutional Amendment Act, 1976, on the recommendation of the Sardar Swaran Singh Committee, and specified ten Fundamental Duties.
- **C.** The Fundamental Duties were inserted by the 44th Constitutional Amendment Act, 1978, when the Janata Government reversed several emergency-era changes and simultaneously introduced a citizen-duty chapter of its own.
- **D.** The duties were introduced through a Supreme Court judgment reading them into Article 21 as necessary corollaries of the right to life, and were codified by Parliament only afterwards to give the judicial formulation a textual home.

**Answer:** B

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — wrong.** The original Constitution contained fundamental rights but not fundamental duties. The framers incorporated duties of the *State* as Directive Principles, and citizen duties were added only in 1976.
- **B — correct.** The 42nd Amendment added Part IVA, consisting of the single Article 51A, which "for the first time specified a code of ten fundamental duties of the citizens", following the Swaran Singh Committee's recommendation for a separate chapter on the subject.
- **C — wrong.** The 43rd and 44th Amendments of 1977 and 1978 undid several 42nd Amendment changes, but the new Janata Government "did not annul the Fundamental Duties", which is evidence of the eventual consensus on retaining them.
- **D — wrong.** The duties were inserted by constitutional amendment, not read in by judicial interpretation. The source records a legislative history, including which of the committee's suggestions Parliament declined to accept.

> **Trap:** The 42nd/44th pairing is the standard confusion. Fix it by the direction of travel: the 42nd *added* Part IVA in 1976, and the 44th, which reversed much else from the emergency period, left the duties untouched.

---

#### MCQ 7

The 86th Constitutional Amendment Act, 2002, is directly relevant to the "role of educational institutions in inculcating values". Which statement most accurately describes what it did?

- **A.** It made value education a compulsory subject in all schools recognised by the appropriate government, prescribing both the syllabus and the minimum instructional hours to be devoted to it at the elementary stage.
- **B.** It transferred education from the Concurrent List to the State List so that value curricula could be locally determined, reversing the position established when the subject was moved to the Concurrent List in 1976.
- **C.** It added Article 21A, making elementary education a fundamental right, and added the eleventh Fundamental Duty, Article 51A(k), on providing educational opportunities to a child or ward aged between six and fourteen years.
- **D.** It made the Fundamental Duties justiciable to the limited extent of the duty relating to education, so that a parent's failure to send a child to school became directly enforceable at the instance of the State.

**Answer:** C

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — wrong.** No constitutional amendment prescribes school subjects. Curriculum is an executive and statutory matter, and the amendment operated at the level of right and duty rather than syllabus.
- **B — wrong.** Education moved to the Concurrent List by the 42nd Amendment in 1976, and the 86th Amendment did not reverse that. Nothing in the 2002 amendment altered the legislative-list position.
- **C — correct.** The same amendment created both limbs: the justiciable Article 21A, listed among the rights to freedom as the right to elementary education, and the eleventh duty at Article 51A(k), addressed to the parent or guardian for the six-to-fourteen age group.
- **D — wrong.** The Fundamental Duties remain non-justiciable in their entirety. There is no partial justiciability, and the source states plainly that the Constitution provides no direct enforcement of any of them by the courts.

> **Trap:** Because Article 21A and Article 51A(k) cover the same age group and arrived together, candidates often assume they have the same legal force. One is a fundamental right and enforceable; the other is a duty and is not.

---

#### MCQ 8

The Sardar Swaran Singh Committee made certain recommendations on Fundamental Duties which Parliament did **not** accept. Which of the following was among the rejected recommendations?

- **A.** That the duties should be placed in a separate part of the Constitution rather than among the Directive Principles of State Policy, so that obligations resting on citizens would not be confused with obligations resting on the State.
- **B.** That the number of duties should be limited to eight rather than expanded further, on the reasoning that a short and memorable list would be more likely to enter public consciousness than a longer catalogue.
- **C.** That the duties should apply to citizens rather than to all persons resident in India, so that foreigners lawfully present in the country would not be placed under obligations owed to a polity of which they are not members.
- **D.** That Parliament may impose a penalty or punishment for non-compliance, that no such penalty law should be questioned in any court on fundamental-rights grounds, and that paying taxes should be a Fundamental Duty.

**Answer:** D

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — wrong.** This recommendation was accepted. The committee asked for a separate chapter, and the 42nd Amendment created Part IVA precisely to house Article 51A.
- **B — wrong.** The committee suggested eight duties and Parliament enacted ten, so its number was exceeded rather than rejected as a proposal to be limited. The eleventh followed in 2002.
- **C — wrong.** The duties are in fact confined to citizens and do not extend to foreigners, so this describes the accepted position rather than a rejected recommendation.
- **D — correct.** All three of these were suggested and none was incorporated: a penalty for non-compliance, immunity of such a penalty law from challenge on fundamental-rights or repugnancy grounds, and a duty to pay taxes.

> **Trap:** This is the cleanest available proof that the duties were designed as declared standards rather than commands. A candidate who knows only that the duties are "non-justiciable" can state the conclusion; one who knows the rejected penalty clause can prove it.

---

#### MCQ 9

An officer writes in an appraisal note that his section values "punctuality, courtesy and file discipline". An examiner would regard this list as incomplete in a specific analytical respect. Which respect?

- **A.** The list contains only instrumental values and never names the terminal value — the end-state such conduct is supposed to serve.
- **B.** The list omits statutory duties, and only duties created by statute may be described as values in an official record.
- **C.** The list is drawn from the personal circle when official notes may only refer to values in the constitutional circle.
- **D.** The list mixes intrinsic and extrinsic values, which is impermissible because the two categories cannot be stated together.

**Answer:** A

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — correct.** Punctuality, courtesy and file discipline are all modes of conduct, which is the definition of an instrumental value. The list never says what they are *for* — reliable service delivery, equal access, an auditable decision — and naming that end-state is what supplies the argument.
- **B — wrong.** Values and statutory duties are different categories. Many values, including courtesy in the conduct rules, exist without statutory form, and nothing confines official value language to statute.
- **C — wrong.** The widening-circles cut describes who holds a value; it does not prohibit reference to any circle in an official note. Professional-circle values are exactly what an appraisal note should discuss.
- **D — wrong.** Intrinsic and extrinsic values are routinely stated together. The distinction governs how they may be traded off, not whether they may appear in the same sentence.

> **Trap:** The list is entirely unobjectionable as conduct, which makes the weakness easy to miss. The diagnostic question is always "instrumental to what?", and a list that cannot answer it has not yet made an argument.

---

#### MCQ 10

The Second ARC records the "values" envisaged in a draft Bill then under consideration of the Ministry of Personnel, Public Grievances and Pensions. Which statement about that list is correct?

- **A.** It was enacted in 2007 and now governs the conduct of all central civil servants under statutory authority, replacing the earlier conduct rules with a positively worded value set that is enforceable through the ordinary disciplinary machinery and is supervised by a statutory authority created for the purpose.
- **B.** It belonged to the draft Public Service Bill, 2006, and included allegiance to the ideals enshrined in the Preamble, apolitical functioning, objectivity and impartiality, accountability and transparency in decision-making, and merit-based selection consistent with cultural, ethnic and other diversities.
- **C.** It was a list of prohibited activities rather than of values, and duplicated the CCS (Conduct) Rules, 1964, adding nothing beyond a restatement of existing proscriptions in more contemporary language.
- **D.** It was drawn from the Nolan principles and reproduced their seven items without modification, so that the Indian civil service would be measured against the same standards as holders of public office in the United Kingdom.

**Answer:** B

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — wrong.** The Commission recorded the Bill as being under consideration and expressly decided that detailed examination "will be appropriately made in its forthcoming report on Civil Services Reforms". It is a draft, and must be cited as such.
- **B — correct.** Paragraph 2.7.5 lists exactly these among the salient values envisaged in the draft Public Service Bill, together with good governance as the primary goal, highest ethical standards, economy and avoidance of wastage, a healthy work environment, and communication, consultation and cooperation.
- **C — wrong.** The report draws the opposite contrast: the conduct rules are "generally directed towards cataloguing specific activities deemed undesirable", whereas the Bill lays down "generic expectations ... which are referred to as 'values'".
- **D — wrong.** The Nolan principles appear separately at paragraph 2.2.5 as a United Kingdom statement of standards for holders of public office. The draft Bill's list is distinct in both origin and content, and includes items such as avoidance of wastage that have no Nolan counterpart.

> **Trap:** The strongest distractor is A, because the list reads like enacted law. Anything drawn from paragraph 2.7.5 must carry the status marker "draft Bill, as recorded in January 2007", or the answer asserts a statutory value regime that does not exist.

---

#### MCQ 11

A department's charter prominently displays "integrity and citizen-centricity", yet its promotion board has never once counted a recorded refusal of improper pressure while consistently counting file disposal volume. Which analytical description of this situation is most precise?

- **A.** A conflict between terminal and instrumental values, since disposal volume is the end-state the department is pursuing while integrity is merely the mode of conduct through which that end-state is to be reached.
- **B.** A failure of instruction, remediable by adding a value-education module to the department's induction programme so that officers understand what the charter's commitment to integrity requires of them in practice.
- **C.** A divergence between espoused and enacted values, in which the organisation is effectively teaching throughput because that is the conduct it reinforces.
- **D.** A conflict of interest, since officers derive a direct personal benefit from the promotion decisions to which they are themselves subject, and are therefore judging matters in which they are interested parties.

**Answer:** C

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — wrong.** The cut is misapplied. Disposal volume is not a terminal value; it is an output measure, and integrity is not competing with it as an end-state. The stem describes an inconsistency between two levels of the same organisation's signalling.
- **B — wrong.** Instruction is the weakest transmission mechanism and is not the one that has failed here. Adding a module leaves untouched the appraisal signal that is doing the actual teaching.
- **C — correct.** The espoused value is what the charter prints; the enacted value is what the organisation rewards, tolerates and punishes. Because conduct that is rewarded is repeated, the department is transmitting throughput accurately and continuously, whatever the charter says.
- **D — wrong.** A conflict of interest arises where an official's private interest may improperly influence the discharge of a public duty. A generic promotion incentive applying to everyone is not a conflict of interest in that sense.

> **Trap:** Option B is attractive because it proposes action. The examinable insight is that the appraisal, not the syllabus, is the transmitting instrument here, and no training budget defeats an appraisal form.

---

#### MCQ 12

Which of the following correctly pairs a value-transmission mechanism with the intervention that actually repairs its failure?

- **A.** Reinforcement failure — remedied by publishing a fresh and more clearly drafted statement of organisational values on the department's website and circulating it to every officer by electronic mail.
- **B.** Modelling failure — remedied by increasing the number of hours of classroom ethics instruction for new entrants, so that the standard is stated more often and in greater detail during induction.
- **C.** Instruction failure — remedied by transferring the officers concerned to a different division at short notice, on the view that a change of working environment interrupts the habits acquired in the previous posting.
- **D.** Reinforcement failure — remedied by redesigning the appraisal so that the conduct the organisation claims to value is actually counted in it.

**Answer:** D

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — wrong.** A published statement changes the espoused value only. Reinforcement operates through what is rewarded, so a website statement leaves the failing mechanism untouched.
- **B — wrong.** Modelling failure means the observed conduct of senior actors contradicts the standard. More classroom hours address instruction, and juniors will continue to believe what they see rather than what they are told.
- **C — wrong.** Short-notice transfer is not a repair for an instruction failure and is, in any event, the standard instrument of punitive posting. Instruction failure is remedied by practice-based rather than lecture-based methods.
- **D — correct.** Reinforcement operates through incentives, appraisal, praise and promotion, so the matching repair is to make the claimed value visible in the instrument that officers believe determines their careers.

> **Trap:** Three of the four options propose real administrative actions, which makes them feel plausible. The discriminating question is always whether the proposed action operates on the same mechanism that failed.

---

#### MCQ 13

In the National Colloquium speech reproduced as Annexure-I(2) of the Second ARC's Fourth Report, the family appears in a specific analytical role. Which role?

- **A.** As an enforcement partner: where civil-service employment is highly regarded and brings prestige, dismissal means disgrace for the entire family, so its members have an incentive to discourage corruption.
- **B.** As the principal source of corruption, on the reasoning that family obligations and the expectations of dependants are the main driver of illicit accumulation by officials in a society of extended kinship networks.
- **C.** As a unit whose values the state should regulate directly through a statutory family code of conduct, on the view that private conduct within the household ultimately determines the integrity of public administration.
- **D.** As an institution the report declines to discuss, on the express ground that private relationships fall outside the scope of governance reform and cannot be addressed by an administrative reforms commission.

**Answer:** A

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — correct.** The speech makes exactly this point in the context of penalties, adding that a bar on subsequent private-sector employment "makes the cost of indulging in corruption even higher". The family functions as a reputational amplifier of the official sanction.
- **B — wrong.** The speech attributes corruption to opportunity, unobservability and weak penalties rather than to family obligation. Its diagnostic passages are the Kautilyan propositions and the cross-country evidence, not a claim about familial demand.
- **C — wrong.** Nothing in the report proposes regulating family conduct. Its recommendations concern political funding, offices of profit, public service values, codes of ethics and regulatory design.
- **D — wrong.** The report does discuss the family, in the passage described in option A, so treating it as outside scope misstates the source.

> **Trap:** The mechanism is reputational and conditional, not sentimental. It works only where office carries prestige and where dismissal is credible, which is why an answer that cites it should also state that condition.

---

#### MCQ 14

The same Annexure reports research findings on how societies form norms about official conduct. Which pair of findings does it record?

- **A.** That norms about official conduct are formed principally by mass-media campaigns and public awareness advertising rather than by personal experience, and that trade and professional associations are usually obstacles to anti-corruption enforcement because they exist to protect their members from external scrutiny and disciplinary consequence.
