- **B.** That where people freely interact on a repeated basis they are more likely to form strong and legitimate norms, and that in low-corruption countries social groups such as trade and professional associations have functioned as "law merchants", promulgating codes of good practice and imposing anti-corruption sanctions.
- **C.** That corruption is unrelated to levels of investment, educational attainment or the protection of civil liberties, and varies only with the severity of the criminal penalties a jurisdiction attaches to bribery and embezzlement.
- **D.** That citizens form their view of officials primarily from formal government communication such as press releases, published reports and official advertising, rather than from their own personal dealings at the counter.

**Answer:** B

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — wrong.** Media campaigns are not the mechanism identified, and associations are described positively as norm-setting and sanctioning bodies rather than as obstacles.
- **B — correct.** Both findings appear in the speech: Cooter's result on repeated free interaction generating strong and legitimate norms, and the observation about trade, professional and community groups acting as "law merchants" in low-corruption countries.
- **C — wrong.** The speech surveys the opposite evidence at length — Mauro on investment and human capital, Knack and Keefer on property and contract rights, Wei on foreign direct investment, and Isham, Kaufmann and Pritchett on participation and civil liberties — and separately notes that Thailand's severe penalties coexist with high corruption.
- **D — wrong.** The reported survey and interview research says the reverse: most citizens judge civil servants by social norms learned in everyday interactions.

> **Trap:** Option D states the intuitive opposite of the finding and is the one a candidate reconstructs from memory if the phrase "everyday interactions" has not been retained. That phrase is the entire administrative payoff, because it makes counter-level service quality a value-transmission instrument.

---

#### MCQ 15

Which statement most accurately reproduces the Second ARC's finding at paragraph 1.3 about public perception of anti-corruption efforts?

- **A.** That the public regards anti-corruption interventions as broadly effective but insufficiently publicised, so that the principal deficiency the Commission identifies is one of communication and public information rather than one of institutional intention, investigative capacity or the speed of disciplinary disposal.
- **B.** That public perception is irrelevant to anti-corruption design because it cannot be reliably measured, and that policy should therefore be built exclusively on prosecution statistics and audit findings.
- **C.** That interventions are seen as mere posturing and as handy weapons for partisan political use, that most people regard corruption as inevitable and any effort to fight it as futile, and that this spreading cynicism bodes ill for the democratic system itself.
- **D.** That public cynicism about anti-corruption effort has declined steadily since the enactment of the Prevention of Corruption Act, 1947, as successive legislative and institutional additions accumulated over the decades.

**Answer:** C

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — wrong.** The report's word is "ineffectual", and its complaint is not about publicity but about the absence of "any real intention to bring the corrupt to book".
- **B — wrong.** The Commission treats perception as central. It devotes a paragraph to it, and the annexed speech relies on perception-based indices as the best macro-level data available in a difficult area.
- **C — correct.** Paragraph 1.3 contains each element: posturing, partisan use to harass opponents, corruption regarded as inevitable, effort regarded as futile, and cynicism spreading so fast that "it bodes ill for our democratic system itself".
- **D — wrong.** The report describes cynicism as spreading rather than declining, and nowhere plots a trend against the 1947 Act.

> **Trap:** This paragraph is the primary-source authority for the proposition that society can transmit resignation as effectively as it transmits integrity. An answer on "the role of society in inculcating values" that never concedes the negative direction has used only half the source.

---

#### MCQ 16

Which statement reproduces the Second ARC's position on **when** the values required of a public servant can be formed?

- **A.** That such values are formed principally during the probationary period and can be reliably instilled by a well-designed foundation course, provided the course is long enough, is taught by officers of standing, and is followed by a field attachment in which the standards taught are immediately put into practice.
- **B.** That value formation is complete at the point of recruitment and that in-service training therefore serves no ethical purpose, being useful only for the transmission of technical and procedural knowledge.
- **C.** That values are formed by the incentive structure alone, so that upbringing, schooling and social environment have no measurable bearing on official conduct once a person has entered public employment.
- **D.** That the inculcation of values enabling subordination of the self to a larger societal good, and a spirit of empathy for those needing ameliorative state intervention, are not skills easily imbibed after joining the civil services, and need nurturing across not merely individual lifetimes but successive generations.

**Answer:** D

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — wrong.** This is the position paragraph 2.7.2 rejects. The Commission's phrase is "not skills which could be easily imbibed after joining the civil services", which is a direct denial that post-entry training is where the disposition is created.
- **B — wrong.** The report does not treat in-service measures as pointless; it recommends codes, values stipulated by law, and disciplinary machinery. Its claim is about the difficulty of *forming* the disposition after entry, not about the futility of everything that follows.
- **C — wrong.** The Commission's own paragraph 1.4 sets the incentive account alongside the values-and-character account and uses both, so an exclusive incentive claim misstates its position.
- **D — correct.** This reproduces paragraph 2.7.2 in full, including the generational horizon and the added observation that "the 'right' ethos takes long to evolve".

> **Trap:** The paragraph cuts two ways and a strong answer uses both edges: it argues *for* school-stage value education because the window closes early, and it argues *against* promising results within one policy cycle because the ethos takes generations.

---

#### MCQ 17

A school prints an excellent value-education syllabus, yet its students consistently learn that hierarchy is natural. Which explanation is most consistent with the way educational institutions transmit values?

- **A.** The hidden curriculum — seating, allocation of cleaning tasks, correction of language, and whether the mid-day meal is eaten together — teaches through modelled practice and defeats the printed content where the two diverge.
- **B.** The syllabus must be legally deficient, since Article 51A(h) requires the development of scientific temper and humanism, and a school whose practice defeats that duty is in breach of an enforceable constitutional obligation.
- **C.** Value education can only be delivered by families, so a school's printed syllabus can have no effect in either direction and the observed outcome is entirely attributable to what the students learn at home.
- **D.** The students' prior attitudes are already fixed by the time they reach school, so no school practice of any kind can alter what they learn about hierarchy, equality or their own standing among their peers.

**Answer:** A

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — correct.** The hidden curriculum operates through modelling, which is the strongest of the five transmission mechanisms, and lived practice defeats taught content whenever the two conflict. This is why the layer that is easiest to legislate is the weakest.
- **B — wrong.** Article 51A(h) is a non-justiciable duty addressed to citizens with no legal sanction for breach, so nothing about it makes a school syllabus actionable.
- **C — wrong.** The syllabus places educational institutions alongside family and society precisely because schools do transmit values, and the ARC's paragraph 2.7.2 argument depends on pre-entry formation including schooling.
- **D — wrong.** This overstates fixity to the point of contradicting the topic. Values are revisable, which is the premise of Box 2.7's developmental account and of adult capacity-building programmes alike.

> **Trap:** Option B is a tempting way to convert an ethical observation into a legal one. Any option that makes a Fundamental Duty enforceable is wrong on the constitutional position, whatever else it says.

---

#### MCQ 18

Which observation would best support an inference that an officer has **internalised** the value of honesty rather than merely performed it?

- **A.** She has completed every prescribed integrity training module, signed the departmental integrity pledge, and filed her annual property return within the stipulated time in each year of her service.
- **B.** She corrected an overpayment that was unlikely to be detected, at the cost of missing a disbursement target, and recorded the reason on the file.
- **C.** She has never been the subject of a vigilance complaint during her period of service, and no adverse entry has ever been recorded against her in the confidential portion of her annual appraisal.
- **D.** She scored highly on the ethics component of her recruitment examination and gave well-reasoned answers to the situational questions put to her during the personality test conducted by the Commission.

**Answer:** B

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — wrong.** Completion and signature are observable outputs, which is exactly what value performance produces. They evidence compliance with a requirement, not conduct under low observability.
- **B — correct.** Three features point the same way: the deviation was unlikely to be detected, correcting it was personally costly, and the reason was recorded for later scrutiny. Those are the accepted proxies for conduct that is not being performed for an audience.
- **C — wrong.** Absence of complaint is weak evidence in both directions. It is consistent with integrity, with an absence of opportunity, and with skilled concealment, since the offence is by nature hard to observe.
- **D — wrong.** Examination performance measures articulated judgement at a moment of maximum incentive to display it. It is the least informative of the four indicators.

> **Trap:** Internalisation cannot be directly verified, so the correct answer is always the one that removes observation and adds personal cost. An option that reports certificates, pledges or clean records is reporting the visible layer.

---

#### MCQ 19

A department's file notings have begun to describe an irregular payment as a "customary facilitation adjustment". Which corrosion pathway does this most precisely illustrate, and what is the matching corrective?

- **A.** Normalisation, corrected by increasing the severity of the penalty prescribed for the conduct so that the deviation ceases to appear ordinary to the officers who are asked to record it.
- **B.** Diffused agency, corrected by reducing the number of officers who handle each file so that responsibility for the payment can be attributed to a single identifiable decision-maker.
- **C.** Euphemism, corrected by insisting on accurate naming in the official record, because the internalised prohibition was attached to the honest word.
- **D.** Incrementalism, corrected by abolishing all discretionary payments in the department so that no gradient exists along which an initially trivial deviation could progressively escalate.

**Answer:** C

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — wrong.** Normalisation is the pathway by which a deviation comes to be seen as the prevailing norm; the stem describes a change in language, not in perceived frequency. Severity of penalty is also a weak instrument, as the report's Thailand example shows.
- **B — wrong.** Diffused agency arises where many hands make each participant feel individually insufficient to the wrong. The stem identifies no such dispersal, and reducing handlers does not restore the missing description.
- **C — correct.** Euphemism operates by renaming: once the act is described in language that does not trigger the internalised prohibition, the prohibition is bypassed without ever being consciously overridden. Restoring the honest word restores the trigger.
- **D — wrong.** Incrementalism is the gradual escalation in which no single step presents itself as the moment of decision. Abolishing all discretionary payments is also a disproportionate response that would disable legitimate functions.

> **Trap:** All four pathways are real and all four correctives are real; the question tests whether the candidate can match them. The signature of euphemism is that the *conduct* is unchanged while the *description* has moved.

---

#### MCQ 20

An officer must choose between a family member's legitimate business interest and an impartial decision on a tender. Which sequence best reflects the correct method of resolution?

- **A.** Weigh the personal and public claims honestly against each other, decide in favour of whichever course produces the greater overall benefit once both are taken into account, and record the reasoning on the file.
- **B.** Refer the matter to the affected family member and seek a voluntary withdrawal of the bid before taking any administrative step, so that the conflict is removed by the private party rather than by the officer.
- **C.** Decide the tender strictly on merits without reference to the relationship, and disclose the connection afterwards if and when the outcome is challenged by an unsuccessful bidder or by an audit objection.
- **D.** Recognise that public authority is held in trust for the people, treat the private claim as disqualified rather than merely outweighed, disclose the relationship and recuse from the decision.

**Answer:** D

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — wrong.** Personal balancing is precisely what a conflict of interest disables, because the officer's judgement is the thing under suspicion. The Second ARC's trusteeship formulation removes the private claim from the calculation rather than assigning it a lower weight.
- **B — wrong.** This makes the propriety of an official decision depend on a private party's cooperation and leaves the officer in a decision-making role throughout the interim.
- **C — wrong.** Disclosure triggered by challenge is not disclosure; it is contingency management. The obligation arises when the conflict arises, not when it is discovered.
- **D — correct.** Paragraph 2.2.2 states that all persons holding authority derive it from the people, that public functionaries are trustees, and that authority must be exercised in the public interest, which is why recusal rather than careful self-assessment is the correct response.

> **Trap:** Option A appeals because it is honest, effortful and produces a record. Trusteeship is the reason it is still wrong: a fiduciary does not weigh his own interest more carefully, he removes it.

---

#### MCQ 21

Which statement most accurately describes the provenance of the "Seven Social Sins" as it appears in the Second ARC's Fourth Report?

- **A.** Box 2.8 heads the list "The Seven Social Sins, as quoted by Mahatma Gandhi in 'Young India,' 1925", and prints "leisure without conscience", whereas the *Young India* note of 22 October 1925 reads "pleasure without conscience" and Gandhi presented the list as one sent to him by a correspondent.
- **B.** The Commission composed the list itself as an illustrative device for its chapter on the ethical framework, and attributed it to Gandhi only in order to lend the enumeration a familiar and persuasive authority that a newly drafted list of prohibitions could not have carried on its own.
- **C.** The list appears in the report's Preface as an epigraph introducing the Commission's argument, and is accompanied there by a full citation to the relevant volume of Gandhi's collected works.
- **D.** The report reproduces eight items rather than seven, the eighth being 'rights without duties', and dates the enumeration to 1947 rather than to the mid-1920s when the note was first published.

**Answer:** A

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — correct.** Every element is verifiable in the report and the canonical record: the box heading uses the phrase "as quoted by", the third item is printed as "Leisure without conscience", and the original note carries "pleasure" and introduces the list as a correspondent's.
- **B — wrong.** The Commission reproduces the list in a box attributed to Gandhi and to *Young India*, 1925. Nothing in the report suggests authorship by the Commission.
- **C — wrong.** The Preface epigraph is a different Gandhi passage — on remaking ourselves rather than remaking the world — and Box 2.8 sits in Chapter 2, at report page 45.
- **D — wrong.** The report prints seven items and dates them to 1925. There is no eighth item and no 1947 date.

> **Trap:** The phrase "as quoted by" is easy to read past, and it is the whole provenance point. It supports the disciplined formulation "a list Gandhi published and endorsed" rather than the looser "Gandhi's seven sins".

---

#### MCQ 22

What is the structural feature that makes the Seven Social Sins usable as an administrative diagnostic rather than as a list to be recalled?

- **A.** Each item names a specific statutory offence under Indian law, so that the list functions as a compact penal index against which an administrator can classify an act of misconduct.
- **B.** Each item pairs a legitimate human end with the ethical means that has been detached from it, so the list is a single decoupling test applied seven times.
- **C.** The items are arranged in ascending order of gravity, so that an administrator can grade an instance of misconduct by the position the corresponding item occupies within the enumeration.
- **D.** Each item corresponds to one of the Nolan principles of public life, so that the two lists can be used interchangeably when a candidate is asked about standards for holders of public office.

**Answer:** B

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — wrong.** None of the seven items is a statutory offence. "Knowledge without character" and "worship without sacrifice" have no penal analogue whatever, and the list predates the Prevention of Corruption Act.
- **B — correct.** Politics is a legitimate end and principles the detached means; commerce is legitimate and morality the detached means; science is legitimate and humanity the detached means. Reading the column rather than the list converts recall into a test.
- **C — wrong.** No ordering by gravity is stated or implied, and the report prints the items as a numbered list without any grading scheme.
- **D — wrong.** The Nolan principles are seven in number, which is the only correspondence. They name positive standards for office-holders — selflessness first — and do not map item by item onto Gandhi's decoupling pairs.

> **Trap:** Option D exploits the coincidence that both lists have seven items. Coincidence of number is not correspondence of content, and asserting the mapping would misdescribe both sources.

---

#### MCQ 23

Box 2.7 of the Second ARC's Fourth Report, headed "Evolution of Morality", quotes a claim about how morality develops. Whose statement is it, and what does it assert?

- **A.** Mahatma Gandhi's, asserting that means and ends are inseparable in public conduct and that an end pursued through corrupted means is thereby corrupted itself, however desirable the end may be.
- **B.** Kautilya's, asserting that officials entrusted with custody of the king's money cannot avoid tasting it, just as honey or poison placed on the surface of the tongue cannot avoid being tasted.
- **C.** Swami Vivekananda's, asserting that as knowledge comes the person grows, morality is evolved and the idea of non-separateness begins, and that this unselfishness is the foundation and quintessence of all ethics.
- **D.** Senator Fulbright's, asserting that among so many influential people morality has become identical with legality, so that conduct which offends the spirit of the law escapes censure if it observes the letter.

**Answer:** C

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — wrong.** Gandhi appears in the report at the Preface epigraph, in Box 2.2 on corruption and hypocrisy, and in Box 2.8 on the Seven Social Sins, but not in Box 2.7.
- **B — wrong.** The Kautilyan propositions appear in Annexure-I(2), the National Colloquium speech at report pages 214–215, and not in a numbered box in Chapter 2.
- **C — correct.** Box 2.7 prints exactly this passage and attributes it to Swami Vivekananda, describing morality as evolving with knowledge and locating its foundation in the widening of the self towards non-separateness.
- **D — wrong.** Fulbright appears in Box 2.3, "Need for Ethical Code", on the gap between legality and morality, which is a different box addressing a different problem.

> **Trap:** Box 2.7 is the most useful Vivekananda anchor in this topic because it is inside the primary report and supplies a *theory of value formation*, yet candidates almost always cite the two examination quotations instead — one of which cannot be traced at all.

---

#### MCQ 24

The Second ARC's Annexure-I(2) reproduces five *Arthasastra* propositions. Which statement correctly describes their structure?

- **A.** All five propositions are diagnostic, establishing that corruption among officials entrusted with the custody of public money is effectively universal, that its detection is impossible in principle, and that no administrative remedy for it is available to the sovereign beyond accepting a fixed level of leakage as the price of administration.
- **D.** Three are diagnostic — temptation from custody, unobservable appropriation, and concealed intent — and two are prescriptive and deliberately asymmetric, requiring wrongful gains to be disgorged and the officers changed in their works, while those who increase the king's wealth in just ways are to be made permanent in their offices.
- **C.** Four of the propositions are prescriptive and only one is diagnostic, the passage as a whole being a plan for the permanent covert surveillance of every official who handles the king's revenue.
- **B.** The five propositions concern the duties of the king towards his subjects rather than the conduct of his officers, and are quoted by the Commission to illustrate an early Indian conception of royal accountability.

**Answer:** D

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — wrong.** Two of the five are explicitly remedial. Treating the passage as counsel of despair omits the disgorgement-and-reassignment proposition and the permanence-for-the-honest proposition entirely.
- **D — correct.** The speech quotes the honey-or-poison, fish-drinking-water and path-of-birds propositions as diagnosis, and then the disgorge-and-change and made-permanent propositions as remedy, with the second remedy positive rather than punitive.
- **C — wrong.** The count is reversed and the characterisation is wrong. Nothing in the five propositions proposes permanent surveillance of every official; the fifth proposition rewards honest officers with security of tenure.
- **B — wrong.** The propositions are about officers appointed to carry out works and about those dealing with the king's money. The subject is official conduct, which is why the Commission's chairman quoted them in an anti-corruption address.

> **Trap:** The famous honey-and-poison line is quoted so often alone that the passage acquires a reputation for pure cynicism. The asymmetry — recovery for the dishonest, permanence for the honest — is the examinable design point and is the standard omission.

---

### REMEDIAL DRILLS 25–32

These eight drills target the specific errors this topic reliably produces.

#### MCQ 25

A Collector defers a decision on a contested land alignment indefinitely, explaining that anekantavada teaches that every viewpoint has validity. What is wrong with this reasoning?

- **A.** Anekantavada is an epistemological doctrine about the many-sidedness of reality and the partiality of any single standpoint; it supports structured, bounded consultation as a way of acquiring missing information, and it is not moral relativism and not a warrant for indecision.
- **B.** Anekantavada applies only to religious and metaphysical questions about the nature of reality, and has no application to administrative decisions of any kind, which are governed by rules rather than by standpoints.
- **C.** The Collector should have invoked syadvada instead, which permits a decision to be deferred until every standpoint has been reconciled with every other and a single unconditional statement can finally be made.
- **D.** Nothing is wrong with the reasoning; consultation must continue until all stakeholders agree, since a decision taken over the recorded objection of an affected party cannot be regarded as ethical, and the many-sidedness of the situation is precisely what makes unanimity the appropriate standard for closure.

**Answer:** A

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — correct.** The doctrine claims that reality is many-sided and that no single standpoint captures it whole, which makes consultation an information requirement. It says nothing about the relativity of right and wrong and supplies no reason to postpone a decision indefinitely.
- **B — wrong.** The doctrine's administrative translation is precisely what the 2025 paper's "relevance in the contemporary world" demand invites, and epistemic humility about partial standpoints is directly applicable to a contested public decision.
- **C — wrong.** Syadvada is conditional predication — prefixing an assertion with *syat*, "in some respect" — and its administrative form is stating the standpoint from which a finding holds. It is a discipline of expression, not a licence to defer.
- **D — wrong.** Unanimity is not the standard for a lawful administrative decision, and a consultation without a closing date becomes a queue of vetoes that disadvantages precisely those least able to keep attending.

> **Trap:** This is the single most common error on Mahavir in GS-IV. Fix it with one sentence: many-sidedness concerns the complexity of truth-claims, not the equivalence of right and wrong.

---

#### MCQ 26

Which statement about Mahavir's five great vows is stated with the precision an examiner expects?

- **A.** Mahavir invented brahmacharya as an ethical idea and introduced it into the ascetic discipline for the first time, which is why his tradition enumerates five great vows where Parshvanatha's tradition had enumerated only four, the additional vow marking a genuine doctrinal innovation rather than a change of enumeration.
- **B.** The five Mahavratas are ahimsa, satya, asteya, brahmacharya and aparigraha; Jain tradition contrasts Parshvanatha's four restraints with Mahavir's five, and brahmacharya is best described as the vow Mahavir's tradition separately enumerated, having earlier been understood within aparigraha.
- **C.** The five Mahavratas are ahimsa, satya, asteya, aparigraha and anekantavada, the last of these functioning simultaneously as one of the great vows and as the doctrine of the many-sidedness of truth.
- **D.** There are four Mahavratas in the Jain tradition, and the fifth item commonly listed alongside them is a later addition unconnected with Jain practice, having entered the enumeration through subsequent commentarial literature.

**Answer:** B

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — wrong.** Invention is the overstatement the precision point exists to prevent. The accurate description is separate enumeration of a restraint previously understood within aparigraha, not the origination of an ethical idea.
- **B — correct.** It names the five vows correctly, states the Parshvanatha contrast accurately, and uses the disciplined formulation "separately enumerated" rather than the unsupportable "invented".
- **C — wrong.** Anekantavada is a doctrine about the many-sidedness of truth, not one of the five great vows, and substituting it drops brahmacharya from the list.
- **D — wrong.** The tradition enumerates five Mahavratas for Mahavir. The four-restraint enumeration belongs to Parshvanatha, and the fifth is not an unconnected later addition.

> **Trap:** Both overstatement (option A) and understatement (option D) are wrong. The examinable formulation sits between them, and the operative phrase is "separately enumerated".

---

#### MCQ 27

A vigilance officer proposes continuous covert monitoring of all officers handling public money, citing Kautilya's observation that one who deals with the king's money cannot avoid tasting it. What is the strongest objection drawn from the source itself?

- **A.** The proposition is not actually found in the Arthasastra at all and was interpolated into the Commission's annexure from a secondary compilation, so nothing in the primary text supports the proposal.
- **B.** Kautilya was writing about revenue farming by private contractors rather than about salaried officials of a permanent bureaucracy, so the analogy between his officers and a modern treasury cadre fails at its root, and any monitoring regime built on the comparison rests on a category mistake about the nature of the office.
- **C.** The same passage quoted by the Second ARC contains a fifth proposition requiring that those who increase the king's wealth in just ways be made permanent in their offices, so the source prescribes an asymmetric regime of positive incentive for honesty alongside recovery from the dishonest, rather than uniform suspicion.
- **D.** Covert monitoring cannot be objected to on ethical grounds at all, because it detects wrongdoing more efficiently than any alternative and efficiency of detection is the decisive consideration in vigilance design.

**Answer:** C

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — wrong.** The proposition is quoted directly in the Chairman's National Colloquium address as reproduced in Annexure-I(2), and it corresponds to Kangle 2.9.32. There is no interpolation to allege.
- **B — wrong.** The propositions expressly concern "one dealing with the money of the king" and "officers appointed for carrying out works", so they are about officials, and this objection misdescribes the text.
- **C — correct.** The objection is internal to the source, which is what makes it strong: the same five-proposition passage pairs disgorgement and reassignment for the dishonest with permanence in office for the honest, so quoting only the temptation premise misrepresents the design.
- **D — wrong.** Efficiency of detection does not settle the question. Blanket monitoring imposes real costs — demoralisation, decision-avoidance, and disproportionate burden on officers who leave records — which is why the asymmetric design matters.

> **Trap:** The strongest objection to a selective quotation is almost always the rest of the same quotation. Reaching for an external principle when the source itself supplies the correction is a weaker move and scores less.

---

#### MCQ 28

Among the following quotations used in this topic, which one is traceable to the thinker's own primary corpus rather than resting on an untraceable attribution?

- **A.** 'Faith is of no avail in the absence of strength. Faith and strength, both are essential to accomplish any great work', attributed by the 2024 paper to Sardar Patel and traceable to his correspondence of the period.
- **B.** 'The greatest discovery of my generation is that a human being can alter his life by altering his attitudes', attributed by the 2025 paper to William James and traceable to his published writings on pragmatism.
- **C.** 'Ethics is knowing the difference between what you have a right to do and what is right to do', widely attributed to Justice Potter Stewart and traceable to a concurring opinion of the United States Supreme Court.
- **D.** "Learn everything that is good from others, but bring it in, and in your own way absorb it, do not become others", set by the 2024 paper and traceable to the *Complete Works*, Volume III, "The Common Bases of Hinduism", Lahore, 1897.

**Answer:** D

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — wrong.** No primary speech or letter has been traced for this line. It is the paper's attribution, and an answer should say so in a clause while addressing the idea of conviction joined to capacity.
- **B — wrong.** The line is widely attributed to James but is not traceable to his writings, having been popularised by Norman Vincent Peale in 1952. A genuine James statement of the theme, from 1895, is available as a substitute.
- **C — wrong.** No primary judicial opinion, speech or writing containing this line has been traced. The documented analogue is the Fulbright passage in the report's Box 2.3 on the gap between the spirit and the letter of the law.
- **D — correct.** This is the one line in the set with an identified primary location, delivered at Lahore in 1897 and preserved in the *Complete Works*.

> **Trap:** Familiarity is not provenance. Three of the four lines here are among the most frequently quoted in Indian ethics preparation, and three of the four cannot be traced. The habit that protects an answer is a three-word label, not a longer memory.

---

#### MCQ 29

Which statement about Savitribai Phule's contribution is stated at the level of precision this package supports?

- **A.** With Jyotirao Phule she co-founded India's first girls' school at Bhide Wada, Pune, in 1848, faced social hostility for teaching girls, later worked on widow-remarriage support and against child marriage and caste discrimination, and is conventionally regarded as India's first woman teacher in the modern sense.
- **B.** She founded the Satyashodhak Samaj in 1873 and personally drafted its constitution, which established compulsory education for girls throughout the Bombay Presidency and provided for its enforcement by the Samaj's local branches, thereby converting a private reform initiative into a system of compulsory schooling.
- **C.** She delivered a celebrated address on the education of women whose text is reproduced in one of the boxes of the Second ARC's Fourth Report, alongside the passages quoted there from Gandhi and Vivekananda.
- **D.** Her school was established under a colonial statute which had made elementary education compulsory for girls in Pune district, so that the initiative implemented an existing legal duty rather than creating a new institution.

**Answer:** A

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — correct.** Each element here is recorded in the canonical owner: the co-founding with Jyotirao Phule, Bhide Wada, Pune, 1848, the documented hostility, the later widow-remarriage and anti-child-marriage work, and the conventional description as India's first modern woman teacher.
- **B — wrong.** This attributes a specific organisational and legislative achievement that is not supported by the sources used here, and no such compulsory-education instrument for the Bombay Presidency is established by them.
- **C — wrong.** The Fourth Report's boxes and annexures quote Gandhi, Vivekananda, Fulbright, Kautilya, Confucius, Plato and Solzhenitsyn among others; no Savitribai Phule text appears in it.
- **D — wrong.** The school was a private reformist initiative, and the account turns precisely on institution-building in the absence of state provision rather than on compliance with a statutory duty.

> **Trap:** This topic invites embellishment because the subject is admirable. The disciplined position is to state the verified facts, to record that the local OCR-searchable books contain no substantive passage on her, and to attribute no quotation to her at all.

---

#### MCQ 30

A District Magistrate is personally generous towards applicants from historically excluded communities, yet the scheme's eligibility condition requires three years of documented prior activity, which almost none of them can produce. Which analysis is most consistent with the constitutional-morality approach taught in this topic?

- **A.** The generosity is sufficient, since ethical administration is ultimately a matter of the officer's disposition towards those she serves, and a sympathetic officer will find ways to accommodate deserving applicants.
- **B.** Personal benevolence cannot substitute for institutional justice; the facially neutral eligibility condition is reproducing the existing hierarchy through its effects, and the corrective is to audit and amend the condition, recording the change as a class rule.
- **C.** The condition is lawful and is therefore beyond ethical criticism, since an administrator may not question a validly prescribed criterion and must confine herself to applying it uniformly to every applicant.
- **D.** The problem is one of attitude among the applicants themselves, who should be encouraged through outreach to maintain better documentation in future so that they can satisfy the condition when it is next applied.

**Answer:** B

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — wrong.** This is exactly the substitution the approach rejects. A benevolent officer distributes discretionary favour; she does not remove the exclusion, and the exclusion survives her transfer.
- **B — correct.** The operative test is whether an apparently neutral procedure reproduces the hierarchy in its effects, and the remedy is procedural — audit the condition, identify the excluded class, and amend by a recorded rule so that relief does not depend on knowing the officer.
- **C — wrong.** Lawfulness settles legality, not propriety. The whole legality–ethics distinction in this topic exists because conduct can be lawful and still fail an ethical standard, and an administrator may certainly propose amendment of a criterion she administers.
- **D — wrong.** This relocates a structural exclusion into a deficiency of the excluded, which is the characteristic error the graded-inequality diagnosis is designed to prevent.

> **Trap:** Option A is attractive because kindness is genuinely valuable. The examinable point is narrower and harder: kindness that leaves the excluding procedure intact has not corrected an injustice, it has decorated one.

---

#### MCQ 31

A candidate writes that Mission Karmayogi's reported 7.26 crore course completions demonstrate a measurable improvement in civil-service ethics. What is the precise error?

- **A.** There is no error in the inference, since course completions are the standard official measure of ethical improvement in capacity-building programmes and are reported to Parliament for precisely that purpose.
- **B.** The figure is a cumulative total for the programme since its inception and therefore cannot properly be attributed to any single year, which is the only respect in which the candidate's use of it is defective.
- **C.** Course completions measure reach rather than internalisation; the figure is the reported position as on 6 February 2026 rather than an undated total, and no general appraisal linkage should be asserted without a service-specific order naming its scope.
- **D.** The error is that Mission Karmayogi concerns functional and domain competencies only and has no behavioural component at all, so no inference about conduct can be drawn from any of its reported figures.

**Answer:** C

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — wrong.** Completions are an output measure of participation. Nothing in them speaks to conduct under low observability, which is the only test of internalisation available.
- **B — wrong.** The objection is not about the period covered. The figure is properly reported with its date, and the error lies in the inference drawn from it rather than in its arithmetic.
- **C — correct.** All three corrections belong together: reach is not internalisation, the figure is dated to 6 February 2026, and an appraisal linkage requires an identified order rather than assumption.
- **D — wrong.** The programme's stated design explicitly covers behavioural competencies alongside functional and domain competencies, so denying the behavioural component misstates it.

> **Trap:** Dated official figures are genuinely useful and should be cited. The failure is the inferential leap from participation data to a claim about character, and one clause of limitation converts the citation from a liability into a strength.

#### MCQ 32

A candidate presents the 2024 GS-IV Q3 and 2025 GS-IV Q3 quotation questions as previous-year questions **owned** by the Human Values and Lessons from Leaders topic. Why is this inaccurate, and what is the correct treatment?

- **A.** The questions are fabricated and do not appear in the official papers at all, having been reconstructed from coaching material and then attributed to the Commission's printed question papers.
- **B.** Quotation questions belong to no topic at all, because a quotation can be applied to any part of the syllabus, so ownership cannot meaningfully be asserted for any question of that form.
- **C.** They are owned by this topic, but only in respect of the Indian thinkers among the quotations, the sub-parts quoting Western thinkers being owned by the Western moral philosophers topic instead, so that a single question can be divided between two owners according to the nationality of the thinker quoted.
- **D.** The verified routing ledgers assign both questions to the Indian Moral Thinkers and the Western Moral Philosophers topics; this topic's own routed demands are different questions, so the quotation items must be retained as clearly labelled cross-topic anchors and must not be counted in the owned total.

**Answer:** D

**Option-by-option explanation**

- **A — wrong.** Both questions appear in the locally held official papers and have been read directly, with their verbatim English wording reproduced in the practice section below.
- **B — wrong.** Ownership is assigned in the repository's verified routing ledgers, and the quotation questions are routed there like any other item. The problem is that they are routed elsewhere, not that routing is absent.
- **C — wrong.** Ledger routing operates at the level of the question, not of individual quotations within it, and there is no partial-ownership category.
- **D — correct.** The 2024 and 2025 Q3 items are routed to the Indian Moral Thinkers and Western Moral Philosophers topics. Retaining them as labelled cross-topic anchors preserves their teaching value; counting them as owned inflates the total and conceals the demands this topic actually owns.

> **Trap:** This is an integrity check rather than a content check. Overstating ownership is attractive because the quotation questions are the most recognisable items in the area, and it is precisely why the ledger, rather than familiarity, must decide.
